Kronecker-product random matrices and a matrix least squares problem
Zhou Fan, Renyuan Ma

Abstract
. We study the eigenvalue distribution and resolvent of a Kronecker-product random matrix model A ⊗ I n × n + I n × n ⊗ B + Θ ⊗ Ξ ∈ ℂ n 2 × n 2 A\otimes I_{n\times n}+I_{n\times n}\otimes B+\Theta\otimes\Xi\in\mathbb{C}^{n^{2}\times n^{2}} , where A , B A,B are independent Wigner matrices and Θ , Ξ \Theta,\Xi are deterministic and diagonal. For fixed spectral arguments, we establish a quantitative approximation for the Stieltjes transform by that of an approximating free operator, and a diagonal deterministic equivalent approximation for the resolvent. We further obtain sharp estimates in operator norm for the n × n n\times n resolvent blocks, and show that off-diagonal resolvent entries fall on two differing scales of n − 1 / 2 n^{-1/2} and n − 1 n^{-1} depending on their locations in the Kronecker structure. Our study is motivated by consideration of a matrix-valued least-squares optimization problem min X ∈ ℝ n × n ⁡ 1 2 ​ ‖ X ​ A + B ​ X ‖ F 2 + 1 2 ​ ∑ i ​ j ξ i ​ θ j ​ x i ​ j 2 \min_{X\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}}\frac{1}{2}\|XA+BX\|_{F}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{ij}\xi_{i}\theta_{j}x_{ij}^{2} subject to a linear constraint. For random instances of this problem defined by Wigner inputs A , B A,B , our analyses imply an asymptotic characterization of the minimizer X X and its associated minimum objective value as n → ∞ n\to\infty .



1.
Introduction

In recent years, high-dimensional probabilistic analyses have yielded important
insights into the exact asymptotic behavior of many
optimization problems with random data. We mention, as several examples,
analyses of ridge regression

[
40
,
17
,
18
]
with possibly non-linear random
features
[
20
,
37
,
47
]

and/or in kernelized domains

[
46
,
66
]

using asymptotic random matrix theory, and analyses of optimization problems
arising in contexts of non-linear regression

[
7
,
21
,
64
,
19
,
22
,
63
]
,
classification

[
62
,
49
,
45
,
16
]
, and
variational Bayesian inference
[
32
,
59
,
12
]

using Approximate Message Passing algorithms, Gaussian comparison and
interpolation arguments, and cavity-method techniques.
In most such examples, the behavior of the optimizer
\hat{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
in the
limit
n\to\infty
is characterized by a system of scalar fixed-point
equations, derived via mean-field approximations over an interaction matrix
having a number of random elements much larger than the
dimension
n
of the optimization variable.

Our current work is motivated by the study of large-
n
asymptotics for a
different type of matrix-valued optimization problem, taking the form

(1)

\min_{X\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}}\frac{1}{2}\|XA+BX\|_{F}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\xi_{i}\theta_{j}x_{ij}^{2}\qquad\text{ subject to }\frac{1}{n}\mathbf{v}^{*}X\mathbf{u}=1.

Here,
\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\xi},\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
are deterministic ridge-regularization and
linear constraint parameters, and we will
study a setting of random inputs given by
independent Wigner matrices
A,B\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
.
Notably, the optimization variable
X=(x_{ij})_{i,j=1}^{n}
(with

x_{ij}
denoting the entries of
X
)
has dimension comparable to

A
and
B
. This problem (
1
) may be written
equivalently in terms of the vectorization
\mathbf{x}=\operatorname{vec}(X)\in\mathbb{R}^{n^{2}}
and
diagonal matrices
\Theta=\operatorname{diag}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
and
\Xi=\operatorname{diag}(\boldsymbol{\xi})
, as

\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n^{2}}}\frac{1}{2}\|(A\otimes I+I\otimes B)\mathbf{x}\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\,\mathbf{x}^{*}(\Theta\otimes\Xi)\mathbf{x}\quad\text{ subject to }\frac{1}{n}(\mathbf{u}\otimes\mathbf{v})^{*}\mathbf{x}=1.

Such a problem is paradigmatic of a broader class of nonlinear problems/models
having a Kronecker-product structure: For motivation, let us mention
the matrix spin glass
model
1
1

1

We would like to thank Justin Ko for bringing a model similar to
(
2
) to our attention.

(2)

\displaystyle p(X)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}}\exp(\operatorname{Tr}AX^{*}X+\operatorname{Tr}BXX^{*})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}}\exp(\operatorname{vec}(X)^{*}(A\otimes I+I\otimes B)\operatorname{vec}(X)),\qquad X\in\{\pm 1\}^{n\times n}

defined by independent GOE coupling matrices
A,B\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
,
and the optimization problem

(3)

\min_{X\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}}\|XA+BX\|\quad\text{ subject to }\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{ij}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}x_{ij}=1,\;x_{ij}\geq 0\text{ for all }i,j=1,\ldots,n

defined by (possibly entrywise correlated) Wigner matrices
A,B\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
. The former model (
2
) describes a
disordered spin system on the lattice, with
A,B
representing couplings for the
row and column inner-products of
X
. The latter
problem (
3
) for various choices of matrix norm

\|XA+BX\|
corresponds to popular convex relaxations of combinatorial
optimization problems over permutation matrices
X
that arise in random graph
matching
[
3
,
67
,
1
]
.

As a step towards developing techniques and insight for asymptotic analyses
of these types of Kronecker-structured models, in this work we carry out an
analysis of the simpler linear problem (
1
) using
random matrix theory methods. We will establish a deterministic approximation
with
O_{\prec}(n^{-1/2})
error for the value of the objective (
1
)
at its minimizer
\widehat{X}
, as well as for the value of

n^{-1}{\mathbf{v}^{\prime}}^{*}\widehat{X}\mathbf{u}^{\prime}
for arbitrary deterministic test vectors
\mathbf{u}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
These results are closely related to a deterministic equivalent
approximation for the resolvent of a Kronecker-product random matrix

(4)

Q=A\otimes I_{n\times n}+I_{n\times n}\otimes B+\Theta\otimes\Xi\in\mathbb{C}^{n^{2}\times n^{2}},

which we will refer to as the “Kronecker deformed Wigner model” (in analogy
with the deformed Wigner model
A+\Theta

studied classically in random matrix theory

[
56
,
11
,
43
,
44
,
42
,
41
]
).
A second main focus of our work is to establish sharp quantitative
estimates for the resolvent
G=(Q-zI)^{-1}
of this model at global
spectral scales, i.e. for fixed spectral parameters
z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}
. Writing

G_{ij}=(\mathbf{e}_{i}\otimes I)^{*}G(\mathbf{e}_{j}\otimes I)\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}

and
G_{ij,\alpha\beta}=(\mathbf{e}_{i}\otimes\mathbf{e}_{\alpha})^{*}G(\mathbf{e}_{j}\otimes\mathbf{e}_{\beta})\in\mathbb{C}
for the blocks and entries of this resolvent, we will show the
operator-norm estimates

(5)

\|G_{ii}^{-1}-G_{jj}^{-1}-(\theta_{i}-\theta_{j})\Xi\|_{\mathrm{op}}\prec n^{-1/2},\qquad\|G_{ij}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\prec n^{-1/2}\text{ for }i\neq j,

the entrywise estimates

(6)

G_{ii,\alpha\alpha}-[G_{0}]_{ii,\alpha\alpha}\prec n^{-1/2},\qquad G_{ii,\alpha\beta}\prec n^{-1/2}\text{ for }\alpha\neq\beta,\qquad G_{ij,\alpha\beta}\prec n^{-1}\text{ for }\alpha\neq\beta,i\neq j,

and the bilinear-form estimates

(7)

(\mathbf{u}\otimes\mathbf{v})^{*}[G-G_{0}](\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbf{v}^{\prime})\prec n^{-1/2}\text{ for deterministic unit vectors }\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{u}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{C}^{n},

where
G_{0}\in\mathbb{C}^{n^{2}\times n^{2}}
is a diagonal deterministic-equivalent
matrix. We remark that although this model (
1
)
is a “toy” setting that simplifies many of the additional complexities of
nonlinear models such as (
2
) and
(
3
), some high-level aspects of this model may be
suggestive of properties to be expected also in these nonlinear models,
including a characterization of the large-
n
limit
by a pair of fixed-point equations in an operator algebra rather than over the
scalar field, and a “two-tiered” mean-field structure as reflected in
(
6
), which arises from separate mean-field
approximations over
A
and
B
.

A special case of the optimization problem (
1
) with

\Theta=\Xi=\eta I_{n\times n}
(and correlated Wigner inputs
A
and
-B
) was
previously analyzed in
[
31
]
, in the context of the graph
matching application. We review a central idea of
this previous analysis in Appendix
A
, which,
however, is special to a commutative setting and does not extend more generally.
Here, we instead follow an alternative approach of a two-stage Schur-complement
analysis of the resolvent, in each stage applying, in an operator-algebra
setting, ideas around Dyson fixed-point equations and
fluctuation averaging techniques developed for Wigner-type models in

[
27
,
28
,
24
,
43
]

and for
\mathbb{C}^{k\times k}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
-valued Kronecker
matrix models with fixed dimension
k
in

[
4
,
26
]
. The
application of these methods in our setting of
k(n)=n
seems to require new
ideas, even to obtain optimal quantitative estimates for fixed
spectral parameters on the global scale, and we discuss this further
in Section
1.1
below.

Recently, a breakthrough line of work has obtained sharp operator norm estimates
for polynomial matrix models in Kronecker-product spaces with growing first
dimension
k\equiv k(n)

[
13
,
53
,
55
,
6
,
8
,
54
,
9
]
.
The authors of
[
13
]
developed a new approach for
estimating expected traces of smooth functions of polynomials in

(8)

(A_{1}\otimes I_{n\times n},\ldots,A_{r}\otimes I_{n\times n},I_{k\times k}\otimes B_{1},\ldots,I_{k\times k}\otimes B_{s})

for deterministic matrices
A_{1},\ldots,A_{r}\in\mathbb{C}^{k\times k}
and
independent GUE matrices
B_{1},\ldots,B_{s}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
,
via an interpolation between the GUE and free semicircular variables and a
differential calculus using Gaussian integration-by-parts and the
semicircular Schwinger-Dyson equation. These results implied
an estimate with
O(\frac{k^{2}}{n^{2}\operatorname{poly}(\Im z)})
-error for the expectation of
the Stieltjes transform, and a strong convergence result (i.e. convergence in
operator norm) when
k\ll n^{1/3}
.
This was extended into a full asymptotic expansion in

[
55
]
and from GUE to Haar unitary matrices in

[
53
,
54
]
, with

[
54
]
deducing strong convergence for Kronecker-product
polynomials in Haar
unitary and deterministic matrices having first dimension

k\leq n/\operatorname{poly}(\log n)
. An analogous
strong convergence statement in the Gaussian setting was shown as part of the
general results of
[
6
]
, using a different
interpolation idea. The authors of

[
8
]
showed strong convergence for
(
8
) when
k=n
and
A_{1},\ldots,A_{r},B_{1},\ldots,B_{s}
are
all independent GUE matrices, via a mapping to unitary
matrices, an extension of the asymptotic expansion in

[
55
]
, and a precise analysis of the
large-
|z|
expansion of the expected Stieltjes transform. In

[
9
]
, strong convergence for Kronecker-product
polynomials of Haar-unitary matrices up to dimensions
k\leq\exp(n^{\alpha})

was shown using a different non-backtracking high trace method.

The focus of our work is a bit different from the above,
as we will not address this question of strong convergence for our model, but
instead study the detailed structure of its resolvent in addition to its
spectral measure. We carry out a more classical analysis using the resolvent
calculus, and for our current purposes, we will also not separate the analysis
of the expectation of the resolvent from its fluctuations. (In particular, we
will not establish an estimate for the expected Stieltjes transform that is more
precise than the
O_{\prec}(n^{-1})
scale of its fluctuations, as would be
needed to show strong convergence.) However, we highlight that our analyses
apply directly to non-Gaussian Wigner matrices, and also yield sharp
operator-norm estimates of the form (
5
) for the
n\times n

resolvent blocks, which may be more difficult to obtain via arguments based
solely on large-
|z|
expansions and analytic continuation ideas.

1.1.
Proof ideas

We present here the high-level ideas of the arguments, deferring to Sections

2
and
3
a more detailed description of the
model and notations. Consider first the model

Q=A\otimes I+I\otimes B+\Theta\otimes\Xi\in\mathbb{C}^{n^{2}\times n^{2}}

and denote its resolvent
G=G(z)=(Q-zI)^{-1}
. The strategy will be
to perform a two-stage Schur-complement analysis of this resolvent, first over
the randomness of
A
, and then over
B
.

In the first stage,
conditioning on
B
, the independence structure of
A\otimes I

suggests a Schur-complement analysis at the level of its
n\times n
blocks:
Denoting
G_{ij}=(\mathbf{e}_{i}\otimes I)^{*}G(\mathbf{e}_{j}\otimes I)\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
and
applying standard resolvent identities, we have

(9)

G_{ii}=\Big(a_{ii}I+B+\theta_{i}\Xi-zI-\sum_{r,s}^{(i)}a_{ir}G_{rs}^{(i)}a_{si}\Big)^{-1},\qquad G_{ij}={-}G_{ii}\sum_{r}^{(i)}a_{ir}G_{rj}^{(i)},

where
G_{rs}^{(i)}
is the version of
G_{rs}
setting to 0
the
i^{\text{th}}
row and column of
A
, and
\sum_{r}^{(i)}
is the summation
over indices
r\neq i
.
Applying concentration of the quadratic form in
the first expression and
averaging over
i=1,\ldots,n
, we will eventually obtain an approximate
fixed-point relation for the partial trace

(n^{-1}\operatorname{Tr}\otimes I)G=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}G_{ii}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Big(B+\theta_{i}\Xi-zI-(n^{-1}\operatorname{Tr}\otimes I)G\Big)^{-1}+O_{\prec}(n^{-1}).

Stability of this equation will imply

(10)

(n^{-1}\operatorname{Tr}\otimes I)G=M_{B}+O_{\prec}(n^{-1}),\quad\text{ for the fixed point }M_{B}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Big(B+\theta_{i}\Xi-zI-M_{B}\Big)^{-1}.

In the second stage, we then carry out the analysis over
B
. As
the implicit dependence of
M_{B}
on
B
in (
10
) is rather complex
and makes a direct Schur-complement analysis difficult, we introduce
an explicit operator algebra representation and write

(11)

M_{B}=(\tau\otimes I)\Big[\underbrace{(\mathsf{a}\otimes I+1\otimes B+\Theta\otimes\Xi-z\,1\otimes I)^{-1}}_{=\tilde{\mathsf{g}}}\Big]

for a (
n
-dependent) von Neumann algebra
\mathcal{A}
with trace
\tau
,
containing a semicircular variable
\mathsf{a}
and the subalgebra
\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}

free of
\mathsf{a}
(Lemma
4.3
).
We study the Stieltjes transform
n^{-2}\operatorname{Tr}G\approx n^{-1}\operatorname{Tr}M_{B}
by first taking

1\otimes n^{-1}\operatorname{Tr}
inside
\tau\otimes I
in (
11
),
and applying resolvent identities parallel to (
9
),

(12)

\tilde{\mathsf{g}}_{\alpha\alpha}=\Big(b_{\alpha\alpha}1+\mathsf{a}+\xi_{\alpha}\Theta-z1-\sum_{\gamma,\delta}^{(\alpha)}b_{\alpha\gamma}\tilde{\mathsf{g}}_{\gamma\delta}^{(\alpha)}b_{\delta\alpha}\Big)^{-1},\qquad\tilde{\mathsf{g}}_{\alpha\beta}={-}\tilde{\mathsf{g}}_{\alpha\alpha}\sum_{\gamma}^{(\alpha)}b_{\alpha\gamma}\tilde{\mathsf{g}}_{\gamma\beta}^{(\alpha)},

to analyze
(1\otimes n^{-1}\operatorname{Tr})\tilde{\mathsf{g}}
. This yields

(13)

(1\otimes n^{-1}\operatorname{Tr})\tilde{\mathsf{g}}=\mathsf{m}_{a}+O_{\prec}(n^{-1}),\quad\text{ for the fixed point }\mathsf{m}_{a}=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\Big(\mathsf{a}+\xi_{\alpha}\Theta-z1-\mathsf{m}_{a}\Big)^{-1},

and applying this back to (
11
) gives an approximation for

n^{-2}\operatorname{Tr}G
. These arguments also yield, as direct
consequences, estimates in operator norm for the resolvent
blocks
G_{ii},G_{ij}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
.

In deducing (
10
) from (
9
) and
(
13
) from (
12
), two difficulties arise that do
not occur in usual scalar random matrix models:

(1)

Applying non-commutative concentration inequalities to analyze
(
9
) and (
12
), several of the
terms controlling the scale of fluctuations cannot be bounded
spectrally by the resolvent. For example, a non-commutative Khintchine-type
inequality gives for
G_{ij}
in (
9
)

(14)

\mathbb{E}\|G_{ij}\|_{p}^{p}\prec\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\max\bigg\{\mathbb{E}\bigg\|\bigg(\sum_{r}G_{rj}^{(i)}G_{rj}^{(i)*}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg\|_{p}^{p},\,\mathbb{E}\bigg\|\bigg(\sum_{r}G_{rj}^{(i)*}G_{rj}^{(i)}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg\|_{p}^{p}\bigg\}.

The second term may be directly controlled by
\|G^{*}G\|_{\mathrm{op}}
and a
spectral argument, but the first term is related instead to the spectrum of the
partial transpose
{G^{\mathsf{t}}}=\sum_{i,j}E_{ji}\otimes G_{ij}
, for which a naive
bound gives
\|G^{\mathsf{t}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq n\|G\|_{\mathrm{op}}

[
65
]
.
Applying this naive bound produces a trivial estimate
G_{ij}\prec 1
.
A similar issue arises for the quadratic forms in the expressions of
G_{ii}

and
\tilde{\mathsf{g}}_{\alpha\alpha}
in
(
9
) and (
12
).

(2)

In the infinite-dimensional context of (
12
),
Khintchine-type inequalities only yield estimates in the
L^{p}
-norms

\|\cdot\|_{p}
for
p<\infty
, rather than a dimension-free estimate in the
operator norm, whereas stability of the fixed-point equation (
13
)
is most readily established under perturbations that are
bounded in operator norm.

We address these difficulties by first carrying out the analysis for
|z|

sufficiently large for which the resolvent admits a convergent series
expansion in
z^{-1}
, and furthermore the concentration of errors in
(
9
) and (
12
) may be established by
expanding into elementary tensors and
applying scalar concentration term-by-term. Then, using a quantitative version
of the maximum modulus principle, we obtain a weak high-probability
estimate in the operator norm

\big\|G_{ii}-\big(B+\theta_{i}\Xi-zI-(n^{-1}\operatorname{Tr}\otimes I)G\big)^{-1}\big\|_{\mathrm{op}}<n^{-\alpha}

for any fixed
z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}
and a small (sub-optimal) constant
\alpha>0

depending on
\Im z

(Lemma
4.9
). Such an estimate and the stability of
the fixed-point equation (
10
) is sufficient to deduce

(15)

G_{ii}-\underbrace{(B+\theta_{i}\Xi-zI-M_{B})^{-1}}_{=M_{i}}\prec n^{-\alpha},\qquad G_{ij}\prec n^{-\alpha}.

These estimates (
15
) now enable the application of
non-commutative analogues of fluctuation averaging
techniques
[
25
]
, which we use in conjunction with
an iterative bootstrapping argument to obtain the
optimal estimates for
G_{ii},G_{ij}
as follows: Writing the
first non-spectral term of (
14
) as

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{r}G_{rj}^{(i)}G_{rj}^{(i)*}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{r}\mathbb{E}_{r}[G_{rj}^{(i)}G_{rj}^{(i)*}]+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{r}\mathcal{Q}_{r}[G_{rj}^{(i)}G_{rj}^{(i)*}]

where
\mathbb{E}_{r}
is the partial expectation over row/column
r
of
A
and

\mathcal{Q}_{r}=1-\mathbb{E}_{r}
, fluctuation averaging with (
15
) as
input shows for the second term

n^{-1}\sum_{r}\mathcal{Q}_{r}[G_{rj}^{(i)}G_{rj}^{(i)*}]\prec n^{-3\alpha}
.
Applying a resolvent expansion of the first term

n^{-1}\sum_{r}\mathbb{E}_{r}[G_{rj}^{(i)}G_{rj}^{(i)*}]
then yields

\mathcal{L}_{2}\bigg(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{r}G_{rj}^{(i)}G_{rj}^{(i)*}\bigg)\prec n^{-1}+n^{-3\alpha}

for the linear operator

\mathcal{L}_{2}(X)=X-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}M_{i}XM_{i}^{*}
, with
M_{i}
defined in
(
15
). This type of operator associated to Dyson
fixed-point equations has been studied previously in

[
2
,
4
]
, and we adapt a
Perron-Frobenius argument of
[
2
]
to show quantitative
invertibility of
\mathcal{L}_{2}
for any fixed
z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}
. In our context, we require
invertibility of
\mathcal{L}_{2}
in the
L^{p}
-norm for each
p\in[1,\infty)
, and in
infinite-dimensional settings where
\mathcal{L}_{2}
may not have an exact
Perron-Frobenius eigenvector — we thus carry out this analysis in the

L^{1}
-
L^{\infty}
duality rather than the Hilbert-space setting of

[
2
]
, and appeal to the Riesz-Thorin interpolation
to obtain invertibility for all
p
(Lemma

4.7
).
This shows
n^{-1}\sum_{r}G_{rj}^{(i)}G_{rj}^{(i)*}\prec n^{-1}+n^{-3\alpha}
, which
applied to (
14
) shows
the implication

G_{ii}-M_{i}\text{ and }G_{ij}\prec n^{-\alpha}\Longrightarrow G_{ii}-M_{i}\text{ and }G_{ij}\prec\max(n^{-1/2},n^{-3\alpha/2}).

Iterating this bound gives finally the optimal errors

G_{ii}-M_{i}\prec n^{-1/2}
and
G_{ij}\prec n^{-1/2}
,
and an additional fluctuation averaging
step shows
n^{-1}\sum_{i}G_{ii}-M_{B}\prec n^{-1}
for the partial trace,
as claimed in (
10
).

These arguments extend to show quantitative approximations for bilinear forms
of the resolvent
(\mathbf{u}\otimes\mathbf{v})^{*}G(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbf{v}^{\prime})
, with

O_{\prec}(n^{-1/2})
error. To show the estimate

G_{ij,\alpha\beta}\prec n^{-1}
for off-diagonal entries, we apply a similar
idea as above, deriving from the resolvent identities and a fluctuation averaging
argument, for each fixed
i\neq j
and
\alpha\neq\beta
,

\mathcal{L}_{1}\bigg(\sum_{k}G_{kj}\mathbf{e}_{\beta}\mathbf{e}_{\alpha}^{*}G_{ik}\bigg)\prec n^{-1/2}

where
\mathcal{L}_{1}(X)=X-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}M_{i}XM_{i}
. Quantitative invertibility
of
\mathcal{L}_{1}
follows from differentiation of the fixed-point equation
(
10
), yielding
\sum_{k}G_{kj}\mathbf{e}_{\beta}\mathbf{e}_{\alpha}^{*}G_{ik}\prec n^{-1/2}
, and we will deduce from this the estimate

G_{ij,\alpha\beta}\prec n^{-1}
(Section
5
).
Finally, results for the least-squares
problem (
1
) are obtained via a similar analysis of a linearized
model (Section
6
)

\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{bmatrix}\otimes(A\otimes I+I\otimes B)-i\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}\otimes\Theta\otimes\Xi-i\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}\otimes I\otimes I\in\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}.

1.2.
Notation and conventions

(\mathbf{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{e}_{n})\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
are the standard basis vectors,

E_{ij}=\mathbf{e}_{i}\mathbf{e}_{j}^{*}
are the coordinate basis elements of
\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
,
and
I_{n\times n}
is the identity matrix (omitting the
subscript when the meaning is clear).
For
M\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
,
\|M\|_{F}=(\sum_{i,j}|m_{ij}|^{2})^{1/2}
and
\operatorname{Tr}M=\sum_{i}m_{ii}
are the Frobenius norm and
(unnormalized) trace. We write
U=\operatorname{diag}(\mathbf{u})
for the diagonal matrix with

\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
on its diagonal, and
\mathbf{u}=\operatorname{diag}(U)
for the vector

(U_{11},\ldots,U_{nn})
on the diagonal of
U\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
.

For a von Neumann algebra
\mathcal{X}
, we denote its unit as
1_{\mathcal{X}}
(omitting the
subscript when the meaning is clear).
Scalars
z\in\mathbb{C}
are identified implicitly as elements of
\mathcal{X}
via

z\mapsto z1_{\mathcal{X}}
. We write
\mathsf{x}\geq 0
if
\mathsf{x}\in\mathcal{X}
is self-adjoint and has
nonnegative spectrum.
For
\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y}\in\mathcal{X}
self-adjoint,
\mathsf{x}\geq\mathsf{y}
means
\mathsf{x}-\mathsf{y}\geq 0
.
We write

\Re\mathsf{x}=\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathsf{x}+\mathsf{x}^{*}),\qquad\Im\mathsf{x}=\tfrac{1}{2i}(\mathsf{x}-\mathsf{x}^{*}),\qquad|\mathsf{x}|=(\mathsf{x}^{*}\mathsf{x})^{1/2}

for the operator real and imaginary parts and absolute value. We denote

\mathcal{X}^{+}=\{\mathsf{x}\in\mathcal{X}:\Im\mathsf{x}\geq\epsilon\text{ for some }\epsilon>0\}

as the elements with strictly positive imaginary part (not to be confused with
the positive cone
\{\mathsf{x}\in\mathcal{X}:\mathsf{x}\geq 0\}
).

\|\mathsf{x}\|_{\mathrm{op}}
is the operator norm, and
\|\mathsf{x}\|_{p}=\phi(|\mathsf{x}|^{p})^{1/p}

is the
L^{p}
-norm for a given trace
\phi
.

2.
Model and main results

2.1.
Kronecker deformed Wigner model

Assumption 2.1
.

(a)

A,B\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
are independent
Wigner matrices
(defined
on an underlying probability space
(\Omega,\mathscr{F},\mathbb{P})
)
satisfying
A=A^{*}
and
B=B^{*}
, and having independent entries

(a_{ij},b_{ij})_{i\leq j}
such that, for all
1\leq i<j\leq n
,

\mathbb{E}a_{ii}=\mathbb{E}b_{ii}=\mathbb{E}a_{ij}=\mathbb{E}b_{ij}=0,\qquad\mathbb{E}|a_{ij}|^{2}=\mathbb{E}|b_{ij}|^{2}=1/n

and for all
1\leq i,j\leq n
, each
p\geq 2
and a constant
C_{p}>0
,

(16)

\mathbb{E}|a_{ii}|^{p},\mathbb{E}|a_{ij}|^{p},\mathbb{E}|b_{ii}|^{p},\mathbb{E}|b_{ij}|^{p}\leq C_{p}n^{-p/2}.

(This includes the special case where
A,B
are real and symmetric.)

(b)

\Theta=\operatorname{diag}(\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}

and
\Xi=\operatorname{diag}(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}

are deterministic diagonal matrices satisfying

\|\Theta\|_{\mathrm{op}},\|\Xi\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\upsilon
for a constant
\upsilon>0
.

We study the Kronecker deformed Wigner model

(17)

Q=A\otimes I_{n\times n}+I_{n\times n}\otimes B+\Theta\otimes\Xi\in\mathbb{C}^{n^{2}\times n^{2}}.

For spectral arguments
z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}
, define the resolvent and Stieltjes
transform of
Q
by

\displaystyle G(z)

\displaystyle=(Q-z\,I_{n\times n}\otimes I_{n\times n})^{-1},

\displaystyle m(z)

\displaystyle=n^{-2}\operatorname{Tr}G(z)=(n^{-1}\operatorname{Tr}\,\otimes\,n^{-1}\operatorname{Tr})\Big[(Q-z\,I_{n\times n}\otimes I_{n\times n})^{-1}\Big].

We will use Roman indices for the first tensor factor, Greek indices for the
second, and write the blocks and entries of
G(z)
as

G_{ij}=(\mathbf{e}_{i}\otimes I)^{*}G(\mathbf{e}_{j}\otimes I)\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n},\qquad G_{\alpha\beta}=(I\otimes\mathbf{e}_{\alpha})^{*}G(I\otimes\mathbf{e}_{\beta})\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n},

G_{ij,\alpha\beta}=(\mathbf{e}_{i}\otimes\mathbf{e}_{\alpha})^{*}G(\mathbf{e}_{j}\otimes\mathbf{e}_{\beta})\in\mathbb{C}.

Our main result will establish deterministic equivalent approximations for

G(z)
and
m(z)
. These may be defined
through a free probability construction, in the following setting:

Assumption 2.2
.

(a)

\mathcal{A}
is a von Neumann algebra with unit
1_{\mathcal{A}}
, operator norm

\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{op}}
, and faithful, normal, tracial state
\tau:\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{C}
.
(We review this definition at the start of
Section
3
.)

(b)

\mathcal{A}
contains as a von Neumann subalgebra

\mathcal{M}\equiv\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}

where
I_{n\times n}\in\mathcal{M}\subset\mathcal{A}
coincides with
1_{\mathcal{A}}
,
and
\tau|_{\mathcal{M}}
and
\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{op}}|_{\mathcal{M}}
restrict to the
normalized matrix trace
\frac{1}{n}\operatorname{Tr}
and matrix operator norm on
\mathcal{M}
.

(c)

\mathcal{A}
has two semicircular elements
\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b}

(i.e. satisfying
\tau(\mathsf{a}^{k})=\tau(\mathsf{b}^{k})=\int_{-2}^{2}x^{k}\,\frac{1}{2\pi}\sqrt{4-x^{2}}\,dx
for each
k\geq 1
) which are free of

\mathcal{M}
with respect to
\tau
.

Let
\mathcal{D}\subset\mathcal{M}
be the subalgebra of diagonal matrices,
generated by the diagonal basis elements

\{E_{ii}\}_{i=1}^{n}
, and denote by
\tau^{\mathcal{D}}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{D}
the diagonal
projection

(18)

\tau^{\mathcal{D}}(\mathsf{x})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\tau(\mathsf{x}E_{ii})}{\tau(E_{ii})}E_{ii}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}n\,\tau(\mathsf{x}E_{ii})E_{ii}.

(This is the unique
\tau
-preserving conditional expectation onto
\mathcal{D}
,
in the sense of Lemma
D.1
.)
We implicitly identify
\mathcal{A}
with its representation on an
underlying Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, and denote by
\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A}
the von
Neumann tensor product acting on
\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathcal{H}
. We denote by

\tau\otimes\tau:\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{C}
,

\tau\otimes 1:\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}
,
and
1\otimes\tau:\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}

the unique bounded linear maps satisfying

(\tau\otimes\tau)(\mathsf{x}\otimes\mathsf{y})=\tau(\mathsf{x})\tau(\mathsf{y}),\qquad(\tau\otimes 1)(\mathsf{x}\otimes\mathsf{y})=\tau(\mathsf{x})\mathsf{y},\qquad(1\otimes\tau)(\mathsf{x}\otimes\mathsf{y})=\tau(\mathsf{y})\mathsf{x},

and similarly for

\tau^{\mathcal{D}}\otimes\tau^{\mathcal{D}}
,
\tau^{\mathcal{D}}\otimes 1
, and
1\otimes\tau^{\mathcal{D}}
.

Identifying
\Theta,\Xi\in\mathcal{M}
as elements of
\mathcal{A}
(which are
free of
\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b}
by assumption), in the limit
n\to\infty
, an
approximation of our matrix of interest
Q
in the tracial sense is given by

\mathsf{q}=\mathsf{a}\otimes 1+1\otimes\mathsf{b}+\Theta\otimes\Xi\in\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A}.

For spectral arguments
z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}
, we define its (deterministic,

n
-dependent) resolvent and Stieltjes transform by

\displaystyle\mathsf{g}(z)

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{q}-z\,1\otimes 1)^{-1},

\displaystyle m_{0}(z)

\displaystyle=\tau\otimes\tau[\mathsf{g}(z)]\in\mathbb{C}^{+}.

The deterministic equivalent matrix
G_{0}(z)
for
G(z)
is then given by

G_{0}(z)=(\tau^{\mathcal{D}}\otimes\tau^{\mathcal{D}})[\mathsf{g}(z)]\in\mathcal{D}\otimes\mathcal{D}.

We remark that
G_{0}(z)
is a deterministic diagonal matrix
in
\mathbb{C}^{n^{2}\times n^{2}}
. (We refer to
[
33
, Theorem 4.4]
for a previous example of this type of construction, in a different model.)

In contrast to more classical random matrix models, the above Stieltjes
transform
m_{0}(z)
in general does not seem to admit a simple characterization
in terms of scalar-valued fixed-point equations.
In some previously studied models with variance profiles or
correlated entries, deterministic equivalents for the Stietljes transform
and resolvent may instead be defined via fixed-point equations in a vector or matrix space

[
4
,
26
,
5
]
.
For our model of interest, the most direct characterization of its asymptotic
Stieltjes transform seems to be
via the following fixed-point equation in the operator algebra
\mathcal{A}
, from
which a deterministic equivalent
G_{0}(z)
for its resolvent may also be
constructed.

Proposition 2.3
.

In the setting of Assumption
2.2
, set

\mathcal{A}^{+}=\{\mathsf{x}\in\mathcal{A}:\Im\mathsf{x}\geq\epsilon\text{ for some }\epsilon>0\}
.
For any
z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}
, there exists a unique element
\mathsf{m}_{a}(z)\in\mathcal{A}^{+}
satisfying the fixed-point equation

(19)

\displaystyle\mathsf{m}_{a}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}(\mathsf{a}+\xi_{\alpha}\Theta-z-\mathsf{m}_{a}(z))^{-1}.

Similarly, there exists a unique element
\mathsf{m}_{b}(z)\in\mathcal{A}^{+}
satisfying

(20)

\displaystyle\mathsf{m}_{b}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\mathsf{b}+\theta_{i}\Xi-z-\mathsf{m}_{b}(z))^{-1}.

We have

\displaystyle m_{0}(z)

\displaystyle=\tau[\mathsf{m}_{a}(z)]=\tau[\mathsf{m}_{b}(z)],

\displaystyle G_{0}(z)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\tau^{\mathcal{D}}\big[(\mathsf{a}+\xi_{\alpha}\Theta-\mathsf{z}-\mathsf{m}_{a}(z))^{-1}\big]\otimes E_{\alpha\alpha}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}E_{ii}\otimes\tau^{\mathcal{D}}\big[(\mathsf{b}+\theta_{i}\Xi-z-\mathsf{m}_{b}(z))^{-1}\big].

Qualitative approximations of the resolvent
G(z)
and
Stieltjes transform
m(z)
by the above constructions
G_{0}(z)
and
m_{0}(z)
may
be obtained using standard techniques of free probability theory, such as a
moment expansion of
G(z)
and
m(z)
for large
\Im z
and analytic
continuation to the full upper half-plane. A primary purpose of our
work is to obtain more quantitative estimates that are not readily deduced from
these types of methods. The following theorem states these estimates, which
represent optimal approximations for
G(z)
and
m(z)

on the global spectral scale. Here, the stochastic domination notation

\xi\prec\zeta
(c.f.
[
25
]
)
means
\mathbb{P}[|\xi|>n^{\epsilon}\zeta]\leq n^{-D}
for any fixed
\epsilon,D>0

and all
n\geq n_{0}(\epsilon,D)
; we review this definition
in Section
3.1
.

Theorem 2.4
.

Fix any
\upsilon,\delta>0
. Under Assumptions
2.1
and

2.2
, uniformly over
z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}
with

|z|\leq\upsilon
and
\Im z\geq\delta
, and over
i,j,\alpha,\beta\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
with
i\neq j
and
\alpha\neq\beta
, we have:

(a)

(Stieltjes transform)

(21)

|m(z)-m_{0}(z)|\prec n^{-1}.

(b)

(Resolvent blocks)

(22)

\displaystyle\Big\|G_{ii}^{-1}-G_{jj}^{-1}-(\theta_{i}-\theta_{j})\Xi\Big\|_{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle\prec n^{-1/2},

(23)

\displaystyle\Big\|G_{\alpha\alpha}^{-1}-G_{\beta\beta}^{-1}-(\xi_{\alpha}-\xi_{\beta})\Theta\Big\|_{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle\prec n^{-1/2},

(24)

\displaystyle\|G_{ij}\|_{\mathrm{op}},\|G_{\alpha\beta}\|_{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle\prec n^{-1/2}.

(c)

(Resolvent entries)

(25)

\displaystyle G_{ii,\alpha\alpha}-(G_{0})_{ii,\alpha\alpha}

\displaystyle\prec n^{-1/2},

(26)

\displaystyle G_{ii,\alpha\beta},G_{ij,\alpha\alpha}

\displaystyle\prec n^{-1/2},

(27)

\displaystyle G_{ij,\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle\prec n^{-1}.

(d)

(Bilinear forms)
Uniformly over vectors
\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{u}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
with

\|\mathbf{u}\|_{2},\|\mathbf{v}\|_{2},\|\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\|_{2},\|\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\|_{2}\leq\upsilon
,

(28)

(\mathbf{u}\otimes\mathbf{v})^{*}\big[G-G_{0}\big](\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbf{v}^{\prime})\prec n^{-1/2}.

The estimates (
25
), (
26
), and
(
27
) indicate that the entries of the resolvent fall on three
scales of orders 1,
n^{-1/2}
, and
n^{-1}
, as depicted in the numerical
simulation of Figure
1
. In a setting where

\Theta\otimes\Xi
commutes with
A\otimes I
and
I\otimes B
, the origins and optimality of these estimates may be understood via
a contour integral representation of
G(z)
, as we discuss in
Appendix
A
.
The above theorem illustrates that various properties of
G(z)

for general
\Theta\otimes\Xi
are in fact similar to this commutative case.

Figure 1.
Entrywise modulus of the resolvent
G(z)=(A\otimes I+I\otimes B+\Theta\otimes\Xi-z)^{-1}

at
z=i
, where
n=20
,
A,B
are independent GOE matrices of size
n
,
and
\Theta,\Xi
have independent
\text{Uniform}(-1,1)
diagonal entries.

2.2.
Application to least-squares problem

In the same setting of Assumption
2.1
with real-valued Wigner
matrices
A,B\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
and

\theta_{i},\xi_{j}>0
, consider now the optimization objective

(29)

f(X)=\frac{1}{2}\|XA+BX\|_{F}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\xi_{i}\theta_{j}x_{ij}^{2}.

Given vectors
\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, define its minimizer under a linear constraint

(30)

\widehat{X}=\argmin_{X\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}}f(X)\qquad\text{ subject to }\qquad\frac{1}{n}\mathbf{v}^{*}X\mathbf{u}=1.

Our main results for this optimization problem (
30
) are the following
asymptotic characterization of the minimum objective value
f(\widehat{X})

and the values of linear forms
n^{-1}{\mathbf{v}^{\prime}}^{*}\widehat{X}\mathbf{u}^{\prime}
for deterministic
test vectors
\mathbf{u}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Theorem 2.5
.

Fix any
\upsilon,\delta>0
. Suppose Assumptions
2.1
and

2.2
hold, where
A,B\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
are real-valued.
Associated to
\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{u}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, denote

\mathsf{u}=\operatorname{diag}(\mathbf{u})
,
\mathsf{v}=\operatorname{diag}(\mathbf{v})
,

\mathsf{u}^{\prime}=\operatorname{diag}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime})
, and
\mathsf{v}^{\prime}=\operatorname{diag}(\mathbf{v}^{\prime})
as diagonal matrices in
\mathcal{D}
.
Then, uniformly over
\Theta,\Xi,\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}
defining (
30
) such that

\Theta,\Xi\geq\delta I
and

\upsilon^{-1}\sqrt{n}\leq\|\mathbf{u}\|_{2},\|\mathbf{v}\|_{2}\leq\upsilon\sqrt{n}
:

(a)

(Objective value)

f(\widehat{X})=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{(\tau\otimes\tau)[(\mathsf{u}\otimes\mathsf{v})[(\mathsf{a}\otimes 1+1\otimes\mathsf{b})^{2}+\Theta\otimes\Xi]^{-1}(\mathsf{u}\otimes\mathsf{v})]}+O_{\prec}(n^{-1/2})

(b)

(Linear projection) Uniformly over
\mathbf{u}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with

\|\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\|_{2},\|\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\|_{2}\leq\upsilon\sqrt{n}
,

\frac{1}{n}\,{\mathbf{u}^{\prime}}^{*}\widehat{X}{\mathbf{v}^{\prime}}=\frac{(\tau\otimes\tau)[(\mathsf{u}^{\prime}\otimes\mathsf{v}^{\prime})[(\mathsf{a}\otimes 1+1\otimes\mathsf{b})^{2}+\Theta\otimes\Xi]^{-1}(\mathsf{u}\otimes\mathsf{v})]}{(\tau\otimes\tau)[(\mathsf{u}\otimes\mathsf{v})[(\mathsf{a}\otimes 1+1\otimes\mathsf{b})^{2}+\Theta\otimes\Xi]^{-1}(\mathsf{u}\otimes\mathsf{v})]}+O_{\prec}(n^{-1/2}).

We remark that the above value

(31)

(\tau\otimes\tau)[(\mathsf{u}^{\prime}\otimes\mathsf{v}^{\prime})[(\mathsf{a}\otimes 1+1\otimes\mathsf{b})^{2}+\Theta\otimes\Xi]^{-1}(\mathsf{u}\otimes\mathsf{v})]

may be understood as

(32)

n^{-2}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbf{v}^{\prime})^{*}(\tau^{\mathcal{D}}\otimes\tau^{\mathcal{D}})[(\mathsf{a}\otimes 1+1\otimes\mathsf{b})^{2}+\Theta\otimes\Xi]^{-1}(\mathbf{u}\otimes\mathbf{v}),

where
(\tau^{\mathcal{D}}\otimes\tau^{\mathcal{D}})[(\mathsf{a}\otimes 1+1\otimes\mathsf{b})^{2}+\Theta\otimes\Xi]^{-1}\in\mathbb{R}^{n^{2}\times n^{2}}
is
a deterministic-equivalent approximation for the matrix

[(A\otimes I+I\otimes B)^{2}+\Theta\otimes\Xi]^{-1}
arising in the
vectorization of the objective (
29
), and these expressions
(
31
) and (
32
)
coincide because this deterministic-equivalent matrix is diagonal.

In an asymptotic setting, if the empirical distributions of coordinates of

\Theta,\Xi,\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{u}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}
converge to deterministic limits, then a qualitative
implication of Theorem
2.5
is a characterization
of the almost-sure limit values of
f(\widehat{X})
and

n^{-1}{\mathbf{v}^{\prime}}^{*}\widehat{X}\mathbf{u}^{\prime}
, as summarized in the following corollary.

Corollary 2.6
.

Asymptotically as
n\to\infty
, if the empirical distributions of coordinates
of
\boldsymbol{\theta}=\operatorname{diag}(\Theta)
,
\boldsymbol{\xi}=\operatorname{diag}(\Xi)
, and
\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{u}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}
satisfy

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{\theta_{i},u_{i},u_{i}^{\prime}}\Rightarrow\mathcal{P},\qquad\frac{1}{n}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\delta_{\xi_{\alpha},v_{\alpha},v_{\alpha}^{\prime}}\Rightarrow\mathcal{Q}

weakly for two joint laws
\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q}
on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
then there exist almost-sure limit values

\displaystyle T(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}(\tau\otimes\tau)[(\mathsf{u}\otimes\mathsf{v})[(\mathsf{a}\otimes 1+1\otimes\mathsf{b})^{2}+\Theta\otimes\Xi]^{-1}(\mathsf{u}\otimes\mathsf{v})],

\displaystyle T^{\prime}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}(\tau\otimes\tau)[(\mathsf{u}^{\prime}\otimes\mathsf{v}^{\prime})[(\mathsf{a}\otimes 1+1\otimes\mathsf{b})^{2}+\Theta\otimes\Xi]^{-1}(\mathsf{u}\otimes\mathsf{v})]

depending only on
\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q}
, and almost surely as
n\to\infty
,

f(\widehat{X})\to\frac{1}{2T(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})},\qquad\frac{1}{n}{\mathbf{u}^{\prime}}^{*}\widehat{X}\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\to\frac{T^{\prime}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})}{T(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})}.

2.2.1.
Numerical computation

Since operator fixed-point equations of the form in Proposition
2.3
are not directly amenable to numerical computation,
we provide in this section a procedure for numerically approximating
the values of
T(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})
and
T^{\prime}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})
in Corollary
2.6

using a moment expansion. (This procedure applies equally to
approximate the finite-
n
value of (
31
) rather than its limit

T^{\prime}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})
, upon replacing the expectations in
(
33
–
34
) below by averages under the empirical
measures of coordinates
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{\theta_{i},u_{i},u_{i}^{\prime}}

and
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\delta_{\xi_{\alpha},v_{\alpha},v_{\alpha}^{\prime}}
.)

For each even integer
m\geq 2
, let
\operatorname{NC}_{2,2}(m)
denote the set of ordered
pairs
(\rho_{a},\rho_{b})
where
\rho_{a},\rho_{b}
are non-crossing pairings of two
disjoint even-cardinality subsets of
\{1,2,\ldots,m\}
whose union is all of

\{1,2,\ldots,m\}
. (The pairings
\rho_{a}
and
\rho_{b}
may cross each other,
and either pairing may be empty.) We associate to each
(\rho_{a},\rho_{b})\in\operatorname{NC}_{2,2}(m)
a value
\mathrm{val}(\rho_{a},\rho_{b})
in the following way:

(1)

Let
w_{a}
be the word in the letters
\{\mathsf{a},\mathsf{d}\}
that is obtained by
traversing the elements of
\{1,\ldots,m\}
in sequential order, and writing

\mathsf{d}\mathsf{a}
for odd elements in
\rho_{a}
,
\mathsf{a}\mathsf{d}
for even elements in
\rho_{a}
,
and
\mathsf{d}
for all elements in
\rho_{b}
.

Similarly, let
w_{b}
be the word obtained by writing

\mathsf{d}\mathsf{b}
for odd elements in
\rho_{b}
,
\mathsf{b}\mathsf{d}
for even elements in
\rho_{b}
,
and
\mathsf{d}
for all elements in
\rho_{a}
.

(2)

Define the complement
K(\rho_{a})
of
\rho_{a}
in
w_{a}

as the coarsest non-crossing partition of its letters
\mathsf{d}
,
for which
\rho_{a}\cup K(\rho_{a})
forms a non-crossing partition of all letters
of
w_{a}
. Define similarly the complement
K(\rho_{b})
of
\rho_{b}
in
w_{b}
.

(3)

Finally, let
S_{1}
be the block of
K(\rho_{a})
that
contains the first letter of
w_{a}
, let
T_{1}
be the block of

K(\rho_{b})
that contains the first letter of
w_{b}
, and set

\displaystyle\mathrm{val}(\rho_{a},\rho_{b})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathsf{U}\mathsf{U}^{\prime}\theta^{-(|S_{1}|+2)/2}\Big]\prod_{S\in K(\rho_{a})\setminus S_{1}}\mathbb{E}\Big[\theta^{-|S|/2}\Big]\times

(33)

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathsf{V}\mathsf{V}^{\prime}\xi^{-(|T_{1}|+2)/2}\Big]\prod_{T\in K(\rho_{b})\setminus T_{1}}\mathbb{E}\Big[\xi^{-|T|/2}\Big]

where the expectations are over

(\theta,\mathsf{U},\mathsf{U}^{\prime})\sim\mathcal{P}
and
(\xi,\mathsf{V},\mathsf{V}^{\prime})\sim\mathcal{Q}
.

For
m=0
, we take
\operatorname{NC}_{2,2}(0)
to consist of the single pair

(\rho_{a},\rho_{b})
with
\rho_{a}=\rho_{b}=\emptyset
both as the empty pairing,
and set

(34)

\mathrm{val}(\emptyset,\emptyset)=\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{U}\mathsf{U}^{\prime}\theta^{-1}]\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{V}\mathsf{V}^{\prime}\xi^{-1}].

To illustrate this in an example, suppose
m=6
,
\rho_{a}=\{(1,3)\}
,
and
\rho_{b}=\{(2,6),(4,5)\}
. Then

\displaystyle w_{a}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\mathsf{d}\mathsf{a}}_{1}\underbrace{\mathsf{d}}_{2}\underbrace{\mathsf{d}\mathsf{a}}_{3}\underbrace{\mathsf{d}}_{4}\underbrace{\mathsf{d}}_{5}\underbrace{\mathsf{d}}_{6}=\mathsf{d}\mathsf{a}\mathsf{d}\mathsf{d}\mathsf{a}\mathsf{d}\mathsf{d}\mathsf{d}

\displaystyle w_{b}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\mathsf{d}}_{1}\underbrace{\mathsf{b}\mathsf{d}}_{2}\underbrace{\mathsf{d}}_{3}\underbrace{\mathsf{b}\mathsf{d}}_{4}\underbrace{\mathsf{d}\mathsf{b}}_{5}\underbrace{\mathsf{b}\mathsf{d}}_{6}=\mathsf{d}\mathsf{b}\mathsf{d}\mathsf{d}\mathsf{b}\mathsf{d}\mathsf{d}\mathsf{b}\mathsf{b}\mathsf{d}

Re-numbering the letters of
w_{a}
as
\{1,\ldots,8\}
,
\rho_{a}
now corresponds
to the pairing
\{(2,5)\}
of the letters
\mathsf{a}
, and
K_{a}
is the partition

\{(1,6,7,8),(3,4)\}
of the letters
\mathsf{d}
. Similarly, re-numbering the letters
of
w_{b}
as
\{1,\ldots,10\}
,
\rho_{b}
is now the
pairing
\{(2,9),(5,8)\}
of the letters
\mathsf{b}
, and
K_{b}
is the partition

\{(1,10),(3,4),(6,7)\}
of the letters
\mathsf{d}
. Then
K_{a}
has blocks of sizes

4,2
,
K_{b}
has blocks of sizes
2,2,2
, so

\mathrm{val}(\rho_{a},\rho_{b})=\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{U}\mathsf{U}^{\prime}\theta^{-3}]\mathbb{E}[\theta^{-1}]\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{V}\mathsf{V}^{\prime}\xi^{-2}]\mathbb{E}[\xi^{-1}]\mathbb{E}[\xi^{-1}].

The values
T(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})
and
T^{\prime}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})
then admit the following series
approximations.

Proposition 2.7
.

Let
(\theta,\mathsf{U},\mathsf{U}^{\prime})\sim\mathcal{P}
and
(\xi,\mathsf{V},\mathsf{V}^{\prime})\sim\mathcal{Q}
where
\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q}

are the joint laws on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
of Corollary
2.6
.
Denote
\|\mathsf{U}\|_{\infty}=\max\{|x|:x\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathsf{U})\}
where
\mathrm{supp}(\mathsf{U})
is the
support of
\mathsf{U}
.
Set
\eta=\min\{\sqrt{x_{a}x_{b}}:x_{a}\in\mathrm{supp}(\theta),\,x_{b}\in\mathrm{supp}(\xi)\}
.
Then for any
M\geq 1
and
z>1
,

\displaystyle T^{\prime}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})

\displaystyle=\mathop{\sum_{m=0}^{M-1}}_{m\text{ is even}}\left(\frac{(-1)^{m/2}}{z(z-1)^{m}}\sum_{k=m}^{M-1}\binom{k}{m}\left(\frac{z-1}{z}\right)^{k}\right)\sum_{(\rho_{a},\rho_{b})\in\operatorname{NC}_{2,2}(m)}\mathrm{val}(\rho_{a},\rho_{b})+r_{M}

where

(35)

|r_{M}|\leq\|\mathsf{U}\|_{\infty}\|\mathsf{V}\|_{\infty}\|\mathsf{U}^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\|\mathsf{V}^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\cdot\eta^{-2}\left(\frac{\sqrt{(z-1)^{2}+16\eta^{-2}}}{z}\right)^{M}.

The same result holds for
T(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})
upon replacing
\mathsf{U}^{\prime},\mathsf{V}^{\prime}
in
(
33
), (
34
), and (
35
) by

\mathsf{U},\mathsf{V}
.

In particular, choosing
z=1+16\eta^{-2}
, the remainder is bounded as

|r_{M}|\leq\|\mathsf{U}\|_{\infty}\|\mathsf{V}\|_{\infty}\|\mathsf{U}^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\|\mathsf{V}^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\cdot\eta^{-2}\Big(\frac{z-1}{z}\Big)^{M/2},

so the series in
m
converges geometrically (with faster convergence for larger
values of the regularization
\eta
). Then
T(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})
and
T^{\prime}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})

may be approximated to high numerical accuracy by computing

\sum_{(\rho_{a},\rho_{b})\in\operatorname{NC}_{2,2}(m)}\mathrm{val}(\rho_{a},\rho_{b})
for a small number
of terms
m=0,2,\ldots,M
. A numerical illustration is provided in Figure

2
.

Figure 2.
Values of
1/(2f(\widehat{X}))
obtained from solving
(
30
) across 10 independent realizations (solid dots, with vertical
lines indicating 1 standard deviation) versus the theoretical prediction

T(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})
computed from Proposition
2.7
with
M=12
(dashed
lines). Here
A,B
are GOE matrices of size
n=1000
, we take

\theta_{i}^{-1},\xi_{\alpha}^{-1},u_{i}^{2},v_{\alpha}^{2}\sim\text{Uniform}(0.05,0.5)/k

with
k\in\{1,1.3,3\}
, and the horizontal axis indicates the correlation
between coordinate pairs
(\theta_{i}^{-1},u_{i}^{2})
and between

(\xi_{\alpha}^{-1},v_{\alpha}^{2})
.

3.
Preliminaries

In the remainder of the paper, we prove the preceding results.
We note that the bulk of our analysis is in fact carried out
for a generalized resolvent operator

R=\bigg(H\otimes\mathsf{x}-\sum_{k=1}^{K}D_{k}\otimes\mathsf{x}_{k}\bigg)^{-1}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}\otimes\mathcal{X}

in the setting of an abstract von Neumann algebra
\mathcal{X}
, which we define
more precisely in Section
4
. This analysis may be of independent
interest to models beyond the ones we consider in our work.

We summarize in
this section the technical tools needed for our analyses.
Section

4
contains the core of our main argument for analyzing
the above generalized resolvent
in an operator-algebra setting.
Section
5
completes the analyses for the Kronecker deformed
Wigner model (
17
), and Section
6
completes the
analyses for the least-squares problem (
30
).

Throughout this section and Section
4
,

\mathcal{X}
is a von Neumann algebra acting on a (complex)
Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, having unit
1_{\mathcal{X}}
, operator norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{op}}
,
and a faithful, normal, tracial state
\phi:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{C}
. We recall that this
means
\phi
is a linear functional satisfying

|\phi(\mathsf{x})|\leq\|\mathsf{x}\|_{\mathrm{op}},\qquad\phi(1_{\mathcal{X}})=1,\qquad\phi(\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y})=\phi(\mathsf{y}\mathsf{x})\text{ for all }\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y}\in\mathcal{X},

\phi(\mathsf{x})\geq 0\text{ for all }\mathsf{x}\geq 0,\qquad\phi(\mathsf{x})=0\text{ only if }\mathsf{x}=0,

and
\phi(\sup\mathsf{x}_{i})=\lim\phi(\mathsf{x}_{i})
for any bounded increasing net
\{\mathsf{x}_{i}\}

of elements
\mathsf{x}_{i}\geq 0
in
\mathcal{X}
. We will be working with
\mathcal{X}
-valued random
variables, understood as strongly (i.e. Bochner) measurable functions from
the underlying probability space
(\Omega,\mathscr{F},\mathbb{P})
to the Banach space

(\mathcal{X},\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{op}})
. For a
\mathcal{X}
-valued
random variable
\mathsf{x}
satisfying
\mathbb{E}\|\mathsf{x}\|_{\mathrm{op}}<\infty
, we denote by
\mathbb{E}\mathsf{x}\in\mathcal{X}
its expectation and
\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{x}\mid\mathscr{G}]
its conditional
expectation with respect to a sub-sigma-field
\mathscr{G}\subset\mathscr{F}

(c.f.
[
58
, Chapter 1]
).

3.1.
Stochastic domination

For
p\in[1,\infty)
, we denote the
L^{p}
-norms with respect to
\phi
as

\|\mathsf{x}\|_{p}=\phi\big(|\mathsf{x}|^{p}\big)^{1/p},\qquad|\mathsf{x}|=(\mathsf{x}^{*}\mathsf{x})^{1/2}.

Relevant properties of these norms and
their associated non-commutative
L^{p}
-spaces are reviewed in Appendix

D
. Throughout, we will write
\mathsf{x}\prec\zeta

to mean stochastic domination of the
L^{p}
-norm for each fixed
p\in[1,\infty)
, in the following sense.

Definition 3.1

(Stochastic domination)
.

Let
\mathsf{x}=\{\mathsf{x}(u):u\in U\}
be a
n
-dependent family of
\mathcal{X}
-valued random
variables, and
\zeta=\{\zeta(u):u\in U\}
a corresponding family of
(positive) scalar-valued random variables, where
U
is a

n
-dependent parameter set. We say that

\mathsf{x}\prec\zeta,\qquad\mathsf{x}=O_{\prec}(\zeta)

uniformly over
u\in U_{n}
if, for any fixed
p\in[1,\infty)

and
\epsilon,D>0
, there exists
n_{0}(p,\epsilon,D)>0
such that for all

n\geq n_{0}
,

\displaystyle\sup_{u\in U_{n}}\mathbb{P}\left[\|\mathsf{x}(u)\|_{p}\geq n^{\epsilon}\zeta(u)\right]\leq n^{-D}.

In the scalar setting of a
\mathbb{C}
-valued random variable
\mathsf{x}
, this means

\sup_{u\in U_{n}}\mathbb{P}\left[|\mathsf{x}(u)|\geq n^{\epsilon}\zeta(u)\right]\leq n^{-D}

for some
n_{0}(\epsilon,D)>0
and all
n\geq n_{0}(\epsilon,D)
.

We will often use implicitly the following basic properties of
\prec
.

Lemma 3.2
.

(a)

If
\mathsf{x}(u,v)\prec\zeta(u,v)
uniformly over
u\in U
and
v\in V
,
and
|V|\leq n^{C}
for some constant
C>0
, then uniformly over
u\in U
,

\displaystyle\sum_{v\in V}\mathsf{x}(u,v)\prec\sum_{v\in V}\zeta(u,v)

(b)

If
\mathsf{x}_{1}\prec\zeta_{1}
and
\mathsf{x}_{2}\prec\zeta_{2}

uniformly over
u\in U
, then also
\mathsf{x}_{1}\mathsf{x}_{2}\prec\zeta_{1}\zeta_{2}

uniformly over
u\in U
.

(c)

Suppose
\mathsf{x}\prec\zeta
uniformly over
u\in U
, where
\zeta
is
deterministic,
\zeta>n^{-C}
,
and
\mathbb{E}[\|\mathsf{x}\|_{p}^{k}]\leq n^{C_{p,k}}
for all
p,k\in[1,\infty)
and some constants

C,C_{p,k}>0
. Then
\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{x}\mid\mathscr{G}]\prec\zeta
uniformly over

u\in U
and over all sub-sigma-fields
\mathscr{G}\subseteq\mathscr{F}

of the underlying probability space
(\Omega,\mathscr{F},\mathbb{P})
.

Proof.

The argument is similar to the scalar setting
(c.f.
[
30
, Lemma D.2]
):
For each fixed
p\in[1,\infty)
, by the triangle inequality and Hölder’s
inequality for the
L^{p}
-norm (Lemma
D.2
),

\bigg\|\sum_{v\in V}\mathsf{x}(u,v)\bigg\|_{p}\leq\sum_{v\in V}\|\mathsf{x}(u,v)\|_{p},\qquad\|\mathsf{x}_{1}\mathsf{x}_{2}\|_{p}\leq\|\mathsf{x}_{1}\|_{2p}\|\mathsf{x}_{2}\|_{2p}.

Statements (a) and (b) then follow from a union bound. For (c), by the given
assumptions, for any
p,k\in[1,\infty)
,
\epsilon>0
, and
all
n\geq n_{0}(\epsilon,k,p)
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\|\mathsf{x}\|_{p}^{k}]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}[\|\mathsf{x}\|_{p}^{k}\mathbf{1}\{\|\mathsf{x}\|_{p}^{k}\leq n^{\epsilon/2}\zeta^{k}\}]+\mathbb{E}[\|\mathsf{x}\|_{p}^{k}\mathbf{1}\{\|\mathsf{x}\|_{p}^{k}>n^{\epsilon/2}\zeta^{k}\}]

(36)

\displaystyle\leq n^{\epsilon/2}\zeta^{k}+\mathbb{E}[\|\mathsf{x}\|_{p}^{2k}]^{1/2}\mathbb{P}[\|\mathsf{x}\|_{p}^{k}>n^{\epsilon/2}\zeta^{k}]^{1/2}<n^{\epsilon}\zeta^{k}.

The triangle inequality for
\|\cdot\|_{p}
implies for
\lambda\in[0,1]
that

\|\lambda\mathsf{x}+(1-\lambda)\mathsf{y}\|_{p}\leq\lambda\|\mathsf{x}\|_{p}+(1-\lambda)\|\mathsf{y}\|_{p}
,
so
\mathsf{x}\mapsto\|\mathsf{x}\|_{p}
is continuous and convex. Then
(c.f.
[
58
, Proposition 1.12]
)

(37)

\|\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{x}\mid\mathscr{G}]\|_{p}\leq\mathbb{E}[\|\mathsf{x}\|_{p}\mid\mathscr{G}].

So for any
\mathscr{G}\subseteq\mathscr{F}
,
p\in[1,\infty)
, and

\epsilon,D>0
, fixing
k\geq 1
such that
(k-1)\epsilon>D

and choosing
n\geq n_{0}(p,\epsilon,D)
large enough so that (
36
)
holds,

\mathbb{P}[\|\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{x}\mid\mathscr{G}]\|_{p}>n^{\epsilon}\zeta]\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}[\|\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{x}\mid\mathscr{G}]\|_{p}^{k}]}{n^{k\epsilon}\zeta^{k}}\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}[\|\mathsf{x}\|_{p}\mid\mathscr{G}]^{k}]}{n^{k\epsilon}\zeta^{k}}\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}[\|\mathsf{x}\|_{p}^{k}]}{n^{k\epsilon}\zeta^{k}}<n^{-(k-1)\epsilon}<n^{-D}.

∎

Remark 3.3
.

For the finite-dimensional matrix algebra

(\mathbb{C}^{n\times n},\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{op}},\frac{1}{n}\operatorname{Tr})
, we have

\|X\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq(\operatorname{Tr}(X^{*}X)^{p/2})^{1/p}=n^{1/p}\|X\|_{p}
.
Thus if
X\prec\zeta
, then for any

\epsilon,D>0
and all
n\geq n_{0}(\epsilon,D)
, choosing
p=\max(1,2/\epsilon)
,

\mathbb{P}[\|X\|_{\mathrm{op}}\geq n^{\epsilon}\zeta]\leq\mathbb{P}[n^{1/p}\|X\|_{p}\geq n^{\epsilon}\zeta]\leq\mathbb{P}[\|X\|_{p}\geq n^{\epsilon/2}\zeta]<n^{-D}

so this implies the operator norm bound
\|X\|_{\mathrm{op}}\prec\zeta
.
However, we caution that this implication does not hold in
infinite-dimensional settings, where our notation
\mathsf{x}\prec\zeta
has a weaker
meaning than
\|\mathsf{x}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\prec\zeta
.

3.2.
Khintchine-type inequalities

The following statements may be deduced from the non-commutative Rosenthal
inequalities of
[
39
]
. In the setting of Rademacher variables,
similar Khintchine inequalities have been shown in

[
57
, Eq. (8.4.11)]
and

[
60
, Theorem 6.22]
.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
(\mathsf{x}_{i},\mathsf{y}_{ij}:i,j=1,\ldots,n)
be (deterministic) elements in

\mathcal{X}
, and denote

(38)

\mathsf{Y}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}E_{ij}\otimes\mathsf{y}_{ij}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}\otimes\mathcal{X},\qquad\mathsf{Y}^{\mathsf{t}}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}E_{ji}\otimes\mathsf{y}_{ij}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}\otimes\mathcal{X}

where
\mathsf{Y}^{\mathsf{t}}
is the partial transpose of
\mathsf{Y}
in its first tensor factor.
We equip
\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}\otimes\mathcal{X}
with the trace

n^{-1}\operatorname{Tr}\otimes\phi
and its
L^{p}
-norm

\|\mathsf{x}\|_{p}=((n^{-1}\operatorname{Tr}\otimes\phi)[|\mathsf{x}|^{p}])^{1/p}
.

Let
(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}:i=1,\ldots,n)
be independent
\mathbb{C}
-valued random
variables, satisfying
\mathbb{E}\alpha_{i}=\mathbb{E}\beta_{i}=0

and
\mathbb{E}[|\alpha_{i}|^{p}],\mathbb{E}[|\beta_{i}|^{p}]\leq C_{p}
for every
p\geq 2
and some
constant
C_{p}>0
. Then for all
p\in[2,\infty)
,
there are constants
C_{p}^{\prime},C_{p}^{\prime\prime},C_{p}^{\prime\prime\prime}>0
such that

(a)

\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}\mathsf{x}_{i}\bigg\|_{p}^{p}\bigg]\leq C_{p}^{\prime}\max\bigg\{\bigg\|\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{x}_{i}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{*}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg\|_{p}^{p},\bigg\|\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{*}\mathsf{x}_{i}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg\|_{p}^{p}\bigg\}

(b)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg\|\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}\beta_{j}\mathsf{y}_{ij}\bigg\|_{p}^{p}\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq C_{p}^{\prime\prime}\max\bigg\{\bigg\|\bigg(\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\mathsf{y}_{ij}\mathsf{y}_{ij}^{*}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg\|_{p}^{p},\bigg\|\bigg(\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\mathsf{y}_{ij}^{*}\mathsf{y}_{ij}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg\|_{p}^{p},n\|\mathsf{Y}\|_{p}^{p},n\|\mathsf{Y}^{\mathsf{t}}\|_{p}^{p}\bigg\}

(c)

Suppose
\mathsf{y}_{ii}=0
for each
i=1,\ldots,n
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg\|\sum_{1\leq i\neq j\leq n}\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}\mathsf{y}_{ij}\bigg\|_{p}^{p}\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq C_{p}^{\prime\prime\prime}\max\bigg\{\bigg\|\bigg(\sum_{1\leq i\neq j\leq n}\mathsf{y}_{ij}\mathsf{y}_{ij}^{*}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg\|_{p}^{p},\bigg\|\bigg(\sum_{1\leq i\neq j\leq n}\mathsf{y}_{ij}^{*}\mathsf{y}_{ij}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg\|_{p}^{p},n\|\mathsf{Y}\|_{p}^{p},n\|\mathsf{Y}^{\mathsf{t}}\|_{p}^{p}\bigg\}

Proof.

See Appendix
B
.
∎

3.3.
Fluctuation averaging

Let
\{\mathscr{G}_{i}:i=1,\ldots,n\}
be a collection of sub-sigma-fields in the
underlying probability space
(\Omega,\mathscr{F},\mathbb{P})
.
For a
\mathcal{X}
-valued random variable
\mathsf{x}
with
\mathbb{E}\|\mathsf{x}\|_{\mathrm{op}}<\infty
,
define the projections

\mathbb{E}_{i}[\mathsf{x}]=\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{x}\mid\mathscr{G}_{i}],\qquad\mathcal{Q}_{i}[\mathsf{x}]=(1-\mathbb{E}_{i})[\mathsf{x}]=\mathsf{x}-\mathbb{E}_{i}[\mathsf{x}].

Supposing that
\{\mathbb{E}_{i},\mathcal{Q}_{i}:i=1,\ldots,n\}
all commute, set

\mathbb{E}_{S}=\prod_{i\in S}\mathbb{E}_{i},\qquad\mathcal{Q}_{S}=\prod_{i\in S}\mathcal{Q}_{i}

with the conventions
\mathbb{E}_{\emptyset}[\mathsf{x}]=\mathcal{Q}_{\emptyset}[\mathsf{x}]=\mathsf{x}
.

The following fluctuation averaging statements in the
L^{p}
-norms on
\mathcal{X}
are
similar to those of the scalar setting, see
e.g.
[
24
, Theorem 4.7]

and
[
29
, Lemma A.2]
.

Lemma 3.5

(Fluctuation averaging)
.

Suppose that
\{\mathbb{E}_{i},\mathcal{Q}_{i}:i=1,\ldots,n\}
commute.
Let
\{\mathsf{x}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
and
\{\mathsf{x}_{ij}\}_{1\leq i\neq j\leq n}
be

\mathcal{X}
-valued random variables such that for any
p,k\in[1,\infty)

and some constants
C_{p,k}>0
,
we have
\mathbb{E}[\|\mathsf{x}_{i}\|_{p}^{k}],\mathbb{E}[\|\mathsf{x}_{ij}\|_{p}^{k}]\leq n^{C_{p,k}}

for all
i\neq j
.

(a)

Suppose
\mathbb{E}_{i}[\mathsf{x}_{i}]=0
, and for some
\alpha,\beta>0
and each fixed

l\geq 1
, uniformly over
S\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}
with

|S|\leq l
and over
i\notin S
, we have

(39)

\left\|\mathcal{Q}_{S}[\mathsf{x}_{i}]\right\|_{l}\prec n^{-\alpha-\beta|S|}.

Denote
\beta^{\prime}=\min\{1/2,\beta\}
. Then uniformly over deterministic vectors

\mathbf{u}=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{n})\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}u_{i}\mathsf{x}_{i}\prec n^{-\alpha-\beta^{\prime}}(n\|\mathbf{u}\|_{\infty})

(b)

Suppose
\mathbb{E}_{i}[\mathsf{x}_{i}]=0
, and for some
\alpha>0
and each fixed

l\geq 1
, uniformly over
S\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}
with
|S|\leq l
and over
i\notin S
, we have

(40)

\left\|\mathcal{Q}_{S}[\mathsf{x}_{i}]\right\|_{l}\prec n^{-\alpha-|S|/2}.

Then uniformly over deterministic vectors

\mathbf{u}=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{n})\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}u_{i}\mathsf{x}_{i}\prec n^{-\alpha}\|\mathbf{u}\|_{2}

(c)

Suppose
\mathbb{E}_{i}[\mathsf{x}_{ij}]=\mathbb{E}_{j}[\mathsf{x}_{ij}]=0
, and
for some
\alpha>0
and each fixed
l\geq 1
,
uniformly over
S\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}
with
|S|\leq l

and over
i,j\notin S
with
i\neq j
, we have

(41)

\left\|\mathcal{Q}_{S}[\mathsf{x}_{ij}]\right\|_{l}\prec n^{-\alpha-|S|/2}.

Then uniformly over
(u_{ij})_{i,j=1}^{n}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{i\neq j}u_{ij}\mathsf{x}_{ij}\prec n^{-\alpha}\left(\sum_{i\neq j}|u_{ij}|^{2}\right)^{1/2}

Proof.

See Appendix
C
.
∎

3.4.
Minors and resolvent identities

Let
H\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
and
\mathsf{x}\in\mathcal{X}
be self-adjoint, and let

\mathsf{z}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}E_{ii}\otimes\mathsf{z}_{i}\in\mathcal{D}\otimes\mathcal{X},\qquad\mathsf{z}_{i}\in\mathcal{X}^{+}=\{\mathsf{x}\in\mathcal{X}:\Im\mathsf{x}\geq\epsilon\text{ for some }\epsilon>0\}.

Consider the generalized resolvent

R=(H\otimes\mathsf{x}-\mathsf{z})^{-1}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}\otimes\mathcal{X}.

We note that this generalized resolvent
exists by the following standard lemma,
since
\Im(H\otimes\mathsf{x}-\mathsf{z})={-}\Im\mathsf{z}\leq{-}\epsilon
.

Lemma 3.6

(
[
35
]
, Lemma 3.1)
.

Suppose
\mathsf{x}\in\mathcal{X}
is such that for some
\epsilon>0
, either
\Im\mathsf{x}\geq\epsilon
or
\Im\mathsf{x}\leq-\epsilon
.
Then
\mathsf{x}
is invertible and
\|\mathsf{x}^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 1/\epsilon
.

For an index set
S\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}
, we define
H^{(S)}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
and
R^{(S)}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}\otimes\mathcal{X}
by

H_{ij}^{(S)}=\begin{cases}H_{ij}&\text{ if }i,j\notin S\\
0&\text{ otherwise}\end{cases},\qquad R^{(S)}=(H^{(S)}\otimes\mathsf{x}-\mathsf{z})^{-1},

with
H^{(\emptyset)}=H
and
R^{(\emptyset)}=R
.
We will use the indexing
R_{ij}=(\mathbf{e}_{i}\otimes 1)^{*}R(\mathbf{e}_{j}\otimes 1)
for the

\mathcal{X}
-valued entries of
R
, and the notations

\displaystyle\sum_{i}^{(S)}=\sum_{i\in\{1,...,n\}\setminus S},\qquad\frac{1}{R_{ii}^{(S)}}=(R_{ii}^{(S)})^{-1},\qquad iS=S\cup\{i\}.

Lemma 3.7

(Resolvent identities)
.

Suppose
H\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
and
\mathsf{x}\in\mathcal{X}
are self-adjoint,
and
\mathsf{z}_{i}\in\mathcal{X}^{+}
for each
i=1,\ldots,n
.
For any
S\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}
:

(a)

For any
i\notin S
,
R_{ii}^{(S)}\in\mathcal{X}
is invertible, and

\frac{1}{R_{ii}^{(S)}}=h_{ii}\mathsf{x}-\mathsf{z}_{i}-\mathsf{x}\left(\sum_{r,s}^{(iS)}h_{ir}R_{rs}^{(iS)}h_{si}\right)\mathsf{x}

(b)

For any
i,j\notin S
with
i\neq j
,

\displaystyle R_{ij}^{(S)}

\displaystyle={-}R_{ii}^{(S)}\mathsf{x}\left(\sum_{r}^{(iS)}h_{ir}R_{rj}^{(iS)}\right)={-}\left(\sum_{s}^{(jS)}R_{is}^{(jS)}h_{sj}\right)\mathsf{x}R_{jj}^{(S)}

\displaystyle={-}h_{ij}R_{ii}^{(S)}\mathsf{x}R_{jj}^{(iS)}+R_{ii}^{(S)}\mathsf{x}\left(\sum_{r,s}^{(ijS)}h_{ir}R_{rs}^{(ijS)}h_{sj}\right)\mathsf{x}R_{jj}^{(iS)}.

(c)

For any
i,j,r\notin S
(including
i=j
) with
r\notin\{i,j\}
,

\displaystyle R_{ij}^{(S)}

\displaystyle=R_{ij}^{(rS)}+R_{ir}^{(S)}\frac{1}{R_{rr}^{(S)}}R_{rj}^{(S)},

\displaystyle\frac{1}{R_{ii}^{(S)}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{R_{ii}^{(rS)}}-\frac{1}{R_{ii}^{(S)}}R_{ir}^{(S)}\frac{1}{R_{rr}^{(S)}}R_{ri}^{(S)}\frac{1}{R_{ii}^{(rS)}}.

Proof.

These follow from Schur-complement inversion identities applied to

H^{(S)}-\mathsf{z}=\begin{pmatrix}h_{11}^{(S)}\mathsf{x}-\mathsf{z}_{1}&h_{12}^{(S)}\mathsf{x}&\cdots&h_{1n}^{(S)}\mathsf{x}\\
h_{21}^{(S)}\mathsf{x}&h_{22}^{(S)}\mathsf{x}-\mathsf{z}_{2}&\cdots&h_{2n}^{(S)}\mathsf{x}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
h_{n1}^{(S)}\mathsf{x}&h_{n2}^{(S)}\mathsf{x}&\cdots&h_{nn}^{(S)}\mathsf{x}-\mathsf{z}_{n}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}\otimes\mathcal{X},

which are purely algebraic and identical to those of the scalar setting, see
e.g.
[
27
, Lemma 4.2]
.
∎

3.5.
Maximum modulus principle

We will use the following quantitative version of the maximum modulus
principle, following
[
61
, Appendix A]
.

Lemma 3.8
.

Fix any
a>0
. For each
r\in(-\infty,0)
, define the circle in
\mathbb{C}^{+}

S_{r}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}:|z-ia\tfrac{1+e^{2r}}{1-e^{2r}}|=\tfrac{2ae^{r}}{1-e^{2r}}\}.

Let
f:\mathbb{C}^{+}\to\mathbb{C}
be any analytic function not identically equal to 0, and set

M(r)=\max_{z\in S_{r}}\log|f(z)|.

Then
r\mapsto M(r)
is increasing and convex over
r\in(-\infty,0)
.

Proof.

For any analytic function
g:\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{C}
(not identically 0)
on the unit disk
\mathbb{D}=\{z:|z|<1\}
, the function

r\mapsto\max_{z\in\mathbb{D}:|z|=e^{r}}\log|g(z)|

is increasing and
convex
[
36
, Section 1]
. Fixing
a>0
, consider the conformal mapping

\psi_{a}:\mathbb{D}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{+}
given by

\psi_{a}(z)=ia\frac{1+z}{1-z}
. For any
z\in\mathbb{D}
,

\left|\psi_{a}(z)-ia\frac{1+|z|^{2}}{1-|z|^{2}}\right|=\left|\frac{2a(z-|z|^{2})}{(1-z)(1-|z|^{2})}\right|=\frac{2a|z|}{1-|z|^{2}}\left|\frac{1-\bar{z}}{1-z}\right|=\frac{2a|z|}{1-|z|^{2}},

so
\psi_{a}
maps each circle
\{z\in\mathbb{D}:|z|=e^{r}\}
bijectively to

S_{r}\subset\mathbb{C}^{+}
. The result then follows from applying the above monotonicity
and convexity to the function
g(z)=f(\psi_{a}(z))
.
∎

4.
Analysis of a generalized resolvent

Much of the analysis for Theorem
2.4

may be stated at the level of a generalized resolvent

(42)

R=(H\otimes\mathsf{x}-\mathsf{z})^{-1}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}\otimes\mathcal{X},\qquad\mathsf{z}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}D_{k}\otimes\mathsf{x}_{k}\in\mathcal{D}\otimes\mathcal{X}

where
\mathsf{x},\mathsf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{x}_{K}
are elements of an abstract von Neumann
algebra
\mathcal{X}
, and
H
is a Wigner random matrix.
We will then specialize to
\mathcal{X}=\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
and
\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{A}
for the two
stages of analysis of the Kronecker deformed Wigner model (
17
).
We emphasize that it is important for our arguments to hold when
\mathcal{X}
is
infinite-dimensional, for the analysis in the second stage with
\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{A}
.

We collect here the assumptions of this general setting.

Assumption 4.1
.

There exist constants
\gamma\geq 1
,
\upsilon\geq\delta>0
, and
K\geq 1

such that:

(a)

H\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
is a random Wigner matrix satisfying the
conditions of Assumption
2.1
,
and
D_{1},\ldots,D_{K}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
are
deterministic and diagonal matrices.

(b)

(\mathcal{A},\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{op}},\tau)
is the von Neumann algebra of
Assumption
2.2
with trace
\tau
,
containing the subalgebra
\mathcal{M}\equiv\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}

and a semicircular element, denoted here as
\mathsf{h}
, that is free of
\mathcal{M}
.

(c)

(\mathcal{X},\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{op}},\phi)
is a von Neumann algebra with faithful, normal,
tracial state
\phi:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{C}
,
and
\mathsf{x}\in\mathcal{X}
is self-adjoint and invertible.

(d)

We have

(43)

\|\mathsf{x}\|_{\mathrm{op}},\|\mathsf{x}^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\gamma,\quad\|\mathsf{z}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\sum_{k=1}^{K}\|D_{k}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|\mathsf{x}_{k}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\upsilon,\quad\Im\mathsf{z}=\Im\sum_{k=1}^{K}D_{k}\otimes\mathsf{x}_{k}\geq\delta.

We denote by
\mathcal{D}\subset\mathcal{M}
the subalgebra of diagonal matrices, and

\tau^{\mathcal{D}}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{D}
the diagonal projection (
18
) onto
\mathcal{D}
.
We remark that under this assumption,
\mathsf{z}
admits an equivalent representation

\mathsf{z}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}E_{ii}\otimes\mathsf{z}_{i},\qquad\mathsf{z}_{i}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}[D_{k}]_{ii}\mathsf{x}_{k}\in\mathcal{X}^{+},\qquad\Im\mathsf{z}_{i}\geq\delta,

and we will use these representations of
\mathsf{z}
interchangeably.
We denote the limiting operator for the generalized resolvent
R
by

\mathsf{r}=\left(\mathsf{h}\otimes\mathsf{x}-\mathsf{z}\right)^{-1}\in\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{X},

its projection under
\tau^{\mathcal{D}}\otimes 1

(the
\mathcal{D}\otimes\mathcal{X}
-valued Stieltjes transform of

\mathsf{h}\otimes\mathsf{x}
evaluated at
\mathsf{z}
) by

\mathsf{r}_{0}=(\tau^{\mathcal{D}}\otimes 1)[\mathsf{r}]\in\mathcal{D}\otimes\mathcal{X},

and its projection under
\tau\otimes 1
by

\mathsf{m}_{0}=(\tau\otimes 1)[\mathsf{r}]=(n^{-1}\operatorname{Tr}\otimes 1)[\mathsf{r}_{0}]\in\mathcal{X}.

We use the indexing
R_{ij}=(\mathbf{e}_{i}\otimes 1)^{*}R(\mathbf{e}_{j}\otimes 1)
and

(\mathsf{r}_{0})_{ij}=(\mathbf{e}_{i}\otimes 1)^{*}\mathsf{r}_{0}(\mathbf{e}_{j}\otimes 1)
,
and write
\mathsf{x}\prec\zeta
or
\mathsf{x}=O_{\prec}(\zeta)

for stochastic domination in the
L^{p}
-norms for
p\in[1,\infty)

as discussed in Section
3.1
.

Our main result in this context is the following theorem.

Theorem 4.2
.

Uniformly over

\mathsf{x},\mathsf{z}
satisfying Assumption
4.1
,

(44)

(n^{-1}\operatorname{Tr}\,\otimes 1)[R]-\mathsf{m}_{0}\prec n^{-1}.

Also uniformly over
i\neq j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
,

(45)

\displaystyle R_{ii}-(\mathsf{r}_{0})_{ii}

\displaystyle\prec n^{-1/2},

(46)

\displaystyle R_{ij}

\displaystyle\prec n^{-1/2},

and uniformly over deterministic vectors
\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
with

\|\mathbf{u}\|_{2},\|\mathbf{v}\|_{2}\leq\upsilon
,

(47)

\displaystyle(\mathbf{u}\otimes 1)^{*}[R-\mathsf{r}_{0}](\mathbf{v}\otimes 1)\prec n^{-1/2}.

In the remainder of this section, we prove Theorem
4.2
.
Section
4.1
discusses existence, uniqueness, and stability
of the solution to the relevant operator-valued fixed-point equation.
Section
4.2
proves a preliminary estimate in operator norm

\|R_{ii}-(\mathsf{r}_{0})_{ii}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\prec n^{-\alpha},\qquad\|R_{ij}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\prec n^{-\alpha}\text{ for }i\neq j

for some
\alpha>0
, by conducting the analysis for sufficiently large
|z|

and extending the result to all fixed
z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}
using the maximum modulus
principle. Section
4.3
improves this to the optimal
estimate of
n^{-1/2}
in the
L^{p}
-norms for
p\in[1,\infty)
,
using the non-commutative Khintchine-type inequalities, fluctuation averaging
techniques, and an iterative bootstrapping argument. Section

4.4
concludes the proof of Theorem
4.2
.

All stochastic domination statements of this section are implicitly uniform over

\mathsf{x},\mathsf{z}
satisfying Assumption
4.1
, indices
i,j,k,\ldots\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, subsets
S\subset\{1,\ldots,n\}
having cardinality
|S|\leq l
for any fixed (
n
-independent) value
l\geq 1
, and unit vectors

\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
satisfying
\|\mathbf{u}\|_{2},\|\mathbf{v}\|_{2}\leq\upsilon
.

4.1.
Operator fixed-point equations

In the following we recall a few relevant notions from operator-valued free probability
theory
[
48
, Chapter 9.2]
:
Let
\operatorname{NC}(m)
be the lattice of non-crossing partitions of
\{1,\ldots,m\}
.
Associated to any von Neumann subalgebra
\mathcal{B}
of
\mathcal{Y}=\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{X}

and its conditional expectation
\tau^{\mathcal{B}}:\mathcal{Y}\to\mathcal{B}

(c.f. Lemma
D.1
) is a system of
\mathcal{B}
-valued
cumulants
(\kappa_{\pi}^{\mathcal{B}})_{\pi\in\operatorname{NC}(m)}
, which are
\mathbb{C}
-multilinear maps

\kappa_{\pi}^{\mathcal{B}}:\mathcal{Y}^{m}\to\mathcal{B}
satisfying the free moment-cumulant relation

(48)

\tau^{\mathcal{B}}(\mathsf{y}_{1}\mathsf{y}_{2}\ldots\mathsf{y}_{m})=\sum_{\pi\in\operatorname{NC}(m)}\kappa_{\pi}^{\mathcal{B}}(\mathsf{y}_{1},\mathsf{y}_{2},\ldots,\mathsf{y}_{m})

and the bimodule properties

(49)

\displaystyle\kappa_{\pi}^{\mathcal{B}}(\mathsf{b}\mathsf{y}_{1},\mathsf{y}_{2},\ldots,\mathsf{y}_{m-1},\mathsf{y}_{m}\mathsf{b}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{b}\kappa_{\pi}^{\mathcal{B}}(\mathsf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{y}_{m-1},\mathsf{y}_{m})\mathsf{b}^{\prime},

\displaystyle\kappa_{\pi}^{\mathcal{B}}(\mathsf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{y}_{r}\mathsf{b},\mathsf{y}_{r+1},\ldots,\mathsf{y}_{m})

\displaystyle=\kappa_{\pi}^{\mathcal{B}}(\mathsf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{y}_{r},\mathsf{b}\mathsf{y}_{r+1},\ldots,\mathsf{y}_{m})

for any
\mathsf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{y}_{m}\in\mathcal{Y}
and
\mathsf{b},\mathsf{b}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}
. Fixing a self-adjoint
element
\mathsf{y}\in\mathcal{Y}
, for any invertible
\mathsf{b}\in\mathcal{B}
with
\|\mathsf{b}^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}

small enough, we may define the
\mathcal{B}
-valued Cauchy-transform

G_{\mathsf{y}}^{\mathcal{B}}(\mathsf{b})=\tau^{\mathcal{B}}[(\mathsf{b}-\mathsf{y})^{-1}]=\sum_{m\geq 0}\tau^{\mathcal{B}}[\mathsf{b}^{-1}(\mathsf{y}\mathsf{b}^{-1})^{m}]\in\mathcal{B},

and for any
\mathsf{b}\in\mathcal{B}
with
\|\mathsf{b}\|_{\mathrm{op}}
small enough, we may define the

\mathcal{B}
-valued R-transform

\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{y}}^{\mathcal{B}}(\mathsf{b})=\sum_{m\geq 1}\kappa_{m}^{\mathcal{B}}(\mathsf{y}\mathsf{b},\ldots,\mathsf{y}\mathsf{b},\mathsf{y})\in\mathcal{B}

where
\kappa_{m}=\kappa_{\pi}
for the partition

\pi=\{\{1,\ldots,m\}\}
. Then, for any invertible
\mathsf{b}\in\mathcal{B}
with

\|\mathsf{b}^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}
small enough, these transforms satisfy the Cauchy-R relation

(50)

G_{\mathsf{y}}^{\mathcal{B}}(\mathsf{b})=(\mathsf{b}-\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{y}}^{\mathcal{B}}(G_{\mathsf{y}}^{\mathcal{B}}(\mathsf{b})))^{-1}.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
\mathcal{D}\subset\mathcal{M}\subset\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{X}

be as in Assumption
4.1
. Let
\mathsf{x}\in\mathcal{X}
be self-adjoint,
and let
\mathsf{h}\in\mathcal{A}
be a semicircular element free of
\mathcal{M}
.

(a)

(
\mathcal{D}\otimes\mathcal{X}
-valued fixed point) For any

\mathsf{z}\in(\mathcal{D}\otimes\mathcal{X})^{+}=\{\mathsf{z}\in\mathcal{D}\otimes\mathcal{X}:\Im\mathsf{z}\geq\epsilon\text{
for some }\epsilon>0\}
, there exists a unique solution
\mathsf{s}\in(\mathcal{D}\otimes\mathcal{X})^{+}
to the fixed-point equation

(51)

\mathsf{s}=\left({-}\mathsf{z}-I_{n\times n}\otimes[\mathsf{x}\left(n^{-1}\operatorname{Tr}\otimes 1[\mathsf{s}]\right)\mathsf{x}]\right)^{-1}.

This solution is given by
\mathsf{r}_{0}=(\tau^{\mathcal{D}}\otimes 1)[(\mathsf{h}\otimes\mathsf{x}-\mathsf{z})^{-1}]
.

(b)

(
\mathcal{X}
-valued fixed point) For any
\mathsf{z}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{z}_{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{+}
,
there exists a unique solution
\mathsf{m}\in\mathcal{X}^{+}
to the fixed-point equation

(52)

\mathsf{m}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left({-}\mathsf{z}_{i}-\mathsf{x}\mathsf{m}\mathsf{x}\right)^{-1}.

Setting
\mathsf{z}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}E_{ii}\otimes\mathsf{z}_{i}\in\mathcal{D}\otimes\mathcal{X}
,
this solution is given by
\mathsf{m}_{0}=(\tau\otimes 1)[(\mathsf{h}\otimes\mathsf{x}-\mathsf{z})^{-1}]
.

Proof.

For existence and uniqueness in part (a), we apply the general
result of
[
38
, Theorem 2.1]
: Let
\mathcal{B}
be any

C^{*}
-algebra and denote its right operator half-plane

\mathcal{B}^{\prime}=\{\mathsf{a}\in\mathcal{B}:\Re\mathsf{a}=\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathsf{a}+\mathsf{a}^{*})\geq\epsilon\text{ for some }\epsilon>0\}.

Then for any
\mathsf{b}\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
and any analytic mapping
\eta:\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
that is
bounded on bounded domains of
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
, there exists a unique solution
\mathsf{s}\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
to the equation
\mathsf{s}=[\mathsf{b}+\eta(\mathsf{s})]^{-1}
.

Fixing
\mathsf{z}\in(\mathcal{D}\otimes\mathcal{X})^{+}
, we take

\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{D}\otimes\mathcal{X}
,
\mathsf{b}={-}i\mathsf{z}/2
, and

\eta(\mathsf{s})={-}i\mathsf{z}/2+I\otimes\mathsf{x}(n^{-1}\operatorname{Tr}\otimes 1[\mathsf{s}])\mathsf{x}
, noting that

\Re[\mathsf{b}]=\Im\mathsf{z}/2\geq\epsilon>0
and

\Re\eta(\mathsf{s})=\Im z/2+I\otimes\mathsf{x}(n^{-1}\operatorname{Tr}\otimes 1[\Re s])\mathsf{x}\geq\epsilon>0
for some
\epsilon>0
whenever
\Re s\geq 0
and

\mathsf{z}\in(\mathcal{D}\otimes\mathcal{X})^{+}
, because
\mathsf{x}
is self-adjoint and

n^{-1}\operatorname{Tr}\otimes 1
is a positive map (Lemma
D.1
).
Thus, there exists a unique solution
\mathsf{s}^{\prime}\in(\mathcal{D}\otimes\mathcal{X})^{\prime}
to

\mathsf{s}^{\prime}=\big({-}i\mathsf{z}+I\otimes\mathsf{x}(n^{-1}\operatorname{Tr}\otimes 1[\mathsf{s}^{\prime}])\mathsf{x}\big)^{-1}.

Multiplying by
i
, there exists a unique solution
\mathsf{s}=i\mathsf{s}^{\prime}\in(\mathcal{D}\otimes\mathcal{X})^{+}
to (
51
).

To show that this solution is given by
\mathsf{r}_{0}=(\tau^{\mathcal{D}}\otimes 1)[(\mathsf{h}\otimes\mathsf{x}-\mathsf{z})^{-1}]
, we claim that the map
\mathsf{s}\mapsto I\otimes\mathsf{x}(n^{-1}\operatorname{Tr}\otimes 1[\mathsf{s}])\mathsf{x}
is the
\mathcal{D}\otimes\mathcal{X}
-valued R-transform of
\mathsf{h}\otimes\mathsf{x}
,
and (
51
) is the Cauchy-R relation over
\mathcal{D}\otimes\mathcal{X}
. Namely, for any
\mathsf{s}\in\mathcal{D}\otimes\mathcal{X}
with
\|\mathsf{s}\|_{\mathrm{op}}
small enough,

(53)

\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{h}\otimes\mathsf{x}}^{\mathcal{D}\otimes\mathcal{X}}(\mathsf{s})=I_{n\times n}\otimes\mathsf{x}\big(n^{-1}\operatorname{Tr}\otimes 1[\mathsf{s}]\big)\mathsf{x}.

Indeed, since
\mathsf{h}
is free of
\mathcal{M}
and hence of
\mathcal{D}\subset\mathcal{M}
,
it is readily checked by definition (c.f.
[
48
, Definition
9.5]
) that
\mathsf{h}\otimes\mathsf{x}
is free
of
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{D}\otimes\mathcal{X}
with amalgamation over
1\otimes\mathcal{X}

under the conditional expectation
\tau\otimes 1:\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{X}\cong 1\otimes\mathcal{X}
.
Then by
[
51
, Theorem 3.6]
,
for any
\mathsf{s}\in\mathcal{D}\otimes\mathcal{X}
with
\|\mathsf{s}\|_{\mathrm{op}}
small enough,

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{h}\otimes\mathsf{x}}^{\mathcal{D}\otimes\mathcal{X}}(\mathsf{s})

\displaystyle=\sum_{m\geq 1}\kappa_{m}^{\mathcal{D}\otimes\mathcal{X}}((\mathsf{h}\otimes\mathsf{x})\mathsf{s},\ldots,(\mathsf{h}\otimes\mathsf{x})\mathsf{s},\mathsf{h}\otimes\mathsf{x})

\displaystyle=\sum_{m\geq 1}\kappa_{m}^{1\otimes\mathcal{X}}((\mathsf{h}\otimes\mathsf{x})(\tau\otimes 1)[\mathsf{s}],\ldots,(\mathsf{h}\otimes\mathsf{x})(\tau\otimes 1)[\mathsf{s}],\mathsf{h}\otimes\mathsf{x})

\displaystyle=\sum_{m\geq 1}\left(1\otimes\left[\mathsf{x}\left(n^{-1}\operatorname{Tr}\otimes 1[\mathsf{s}]\right)\right]^{m-1}\mathsf{x}\right)\kappa_{m}^{1\otimes\mathcal{X}}(\mathsf{h}\otimes 1,...,\mathsf{h}\otimes 1)

where we used in the last step that

\tau\otimes 1[\mathsf{s}]=n^{-1}\operatorname{Tr}\otimes 1[\mathsf{s}]

for any
\mathsf{s}\in\mathcal{D}\otimes\mathcal{X}
, that
\mathsf{h}\otimes 1
commutes with
1\otimes\mathcal{X}
,
and the bimodule properties (
49
).
On the other hand, we have

\kappa_{m}^{1\otimes\mathcal{X}}(\mathsf{h}\otimes 1,\ldots,\mathsf{h}\otimes 1)=\kappa_{m}(\mathsf{h},\ldots,\mathsf{h})(1\otimes 1)

where
\kappa_{m}(\mathsf{h},\ldots,\mathsf{h})
are the scalar-valued free cumulants of
\mathsf{h}
:
This may be verified by expressing

\kappa_{m}^{1\otimes\mathcal{X}}(\mathsf{h}\otimes 1,\ldots,\mathsf{h}\otimes 1)

in terms of moments under the conditional expectation
\tau\otimes 1

using Möbius-inversion of
(
48
) (c.f.
[
48
, Eq. (9.19)]
),
applying the identity
(\tau\otimes 1)[\mathsf{a}\otimes 1]=\tau(\mathsf{a})(1\otimes 1)
for
each moment term, and
re-applying (
48
) to express the result back in terms of
the scalar cumulants
\kappa_{m}(\mathsf{h},\ldots,\mathsf{h})
.
Here,
\mathsf{h}\in\mathcal{A}
is semi-circular, so

\kappa_{m}(\mathsf{h},\ldots,\mathsf{h})=1
if
m=2
and 0 otherwise
(c.f.
[
52
, Example 11.21]
). Thus only the
m=2
term above
remains, and we obtain the claim (
53
).

Then, identifying
\mathsf{r}_{0}(\mathsf{z})=(\tau^{\mathcal{D}}\otimes 1)[(\mathsf{h}\otimes\mathsf{x}-\mathsf{z})^{-1}]={-}G_{\mathsf{h}\otimes\mathsf{x}}^{\mathcal{D}\otimes\mathcal{X}}(\mathsf{z})
, the Cauchy-R relation
(
50
) implies that

\mathsf{r}_{0}(\mathsf{z})=\left({-}\mathsf{z}-I\otimes\mathsf{x}\left(n^{-1}\operatorname{Tr}\otimes 1[\mathsf{r}_{0}(\mathsf{z})]\right)\mathsf{x}\right)^{-1}

for all
\mathsf{z}\in(\mathcal{D}\otimes\mathcal{X})^{+}
with
\|\mathsf{z}^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}
small enough.
Since the two sides of this equation are analytic over the operator half-plane

\mathsf{z}\in(\mathcal{D}\otimes\mathcal{X})^{+}

and are equal on an open subset of
(\mathcal{D}\otimes\mathcal{X})^{+}
, it follows from the identity principle
they must be equal for all

\mathsf{z}\in(\mathcal{D}\otimes\mathcal{X})^{+}
, showing part (a) that
\mathsf{r}_{0}
is the unique
solution of (
51
) for any such
\mathsf{z}
.

For part (b), observe that for any
\mathsf{m}\in\mathcal{X}^{+}
,

(\mathsf{z}+I_{n\times n}\otimes\mathsf{x}\mathsf{m}\mathsf{x})^{-1}=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}E_{ii}\otimes(\mathsf{z}_{i}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{m}\mathsf{x})\right)^{-1}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}E_{ii}\otimes(\mathsf{z}_{i}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{m}\mathsf{x})^{-1}.

Then setting
\mathsf{m}_{0}=n^{-1}\operatorname{Tr}\otimes 1[\mathsf{r}_{0}]=(\tau\otimes 1)[(\mathsf{h}\otimes\mathsf{x}-\mathsf{z})^{-1}]
and
taking
n^{-1}\operatorname{Tr}\otimes 1
on both sides of
(
51
) with
\mathsf{s}=\mathsf{r}_{0}
shows that
\mathsf{m}_{0}
solves
(
52
). To see that
\mathsf{m}_{0}
is the unique solution,
observe that if
\mathsf{m}\in\mathcal{X}^{+}

solves (
52
), then
defining
\mathsf{s}_{i}=(-\mathsf{z}_{i}-\mathsf{x}\mathsf{m}\mathsf{x})^{-1}

and
\mathsf{s}=\sum_{i}E_{ii}\otimes\mathsf{s}_{i}\in(\mathcal{D}\otimes\mathcal{X})^{+}
, it follows from
(
52
) that
\mathsf{m}=n^{-1}\operatorname{Tr}\otimes 1[\mathsf{s}]
, so

\mathsf{s}
solves (
51
).
Then by the uniqueness claim of part (a), we must have
\mathsf{s}=\mathsf{r}_{0}
,
so
\mathsf{m}=n^{-1}\operatorname{Tr}\otimes 1[\mathsf{r}_{0}]=\mathsf{m}_{0}
. Hence this solution
\mathsf{m}_{0}\in\mathcal{X}^{+}
to
(
52
) is unique.
∎

We deduce from the above the following stability statements for approximate
solutions of these fixed-point equations.

Corollary 4.4
.

Under Assumption
4.1
:

(a)

Suppose
\mathsf{s}\in(\mathcal{D}\otimes\mathcal{X})^{+}
and
\Delta\in\mathcal{D}\otimes\mathcal{X}

satisfy
\Im(\mathsf{z}+\Delta)\geq\delta/2
and

\mathsf{s}=\left(-\mathsf{z}-\Delta-I_{n\times n}\otimes[\mathsf{x}\left(n^{-1}\operatorname{Tr}\otimes 1[\mathsf{s}]\right)\mathsf{x}]\right)^{-1}.

Then for any
p\in[1,\infty]
(where
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}=\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{op}}
),

\left\|\mathsf{s}-\mathsf{r}_{0}\right\|_{p}\leq 2\delta^{-2}\left\|\Delta\right\|_{p}.

(b)

Suppose
\mathsf{m}\in\mathcal{X}^{+}
and
\Delta\in\mathcal{X}
satisfy

\Im(\mathsf{z}_{i}+\mathsf{x}\Delta\mathsf{x})\geq\delta/2
for all
i=1,\ldots,n
and

(54)

\displaystyle\mathsf{m}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(-\mathsf{z}_{i}-\mathsf{x}\mathsf{m}\mathsf{x}\right)^{-1}+\Delta.

Then for any
p\in[1,\infty]
,

\left\|\mathsf{m}-\mathsf{m}_{0}\right\|_{p}\leq(1+2\gamma^{2}\delta^{-2})\left\|\Delta\right\|_{p}

Proof.

For part (a),
defining
\mathsf{r}(\mathsf{z})=(\mathsf{h}\otimes\mathsf{x}-\mathsf{z})^{-1}
and
\mathsf{r}_{0}(\mathsf{z})=(\tau^{\mathcal{D}}\otimes 1)[\mathsf{r}(\mathsf{z})]
, Lemma
4.3
(a) implies that

\mathsf{s}=\mathsf{r}_{0}(\mathsf{z}+\Delta)
. Hence

\displaystyle\left\|\mathsf{s}-\mathsf{r}_{0}\right\|_{p}
