Recall (c.f. (
107
)) that

(114)

\sum_{\gamma}^{[\alpha]}b_{\alpha\gamma}G_{\gamma\beta}^{[\alpha]}\prec n^{-1/2}.

By (
45
)
applied to the second tensor factor, we have

G_{\alpha\alpha}=M_{\alpha}+O_{\prec}(n^{-1/2})
, where
M_{\alpha}
depends only on

A
and not on
B
. Then by Lemma
3.2
(c), also

\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}G_{\alpha\alpha}=M_{\alpha}+O_{\prec}(n^{-1/2})
, so

(115)

G_{\alpha\alpha}-\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}G_{\alpha\alpha}\prec n^{-1/2}.

Then applying both (
114
) and
(
115
),

G_{\alpha\beta}={-}(\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}G_{\alpha\alpha})\sum_{\gamma}^{[\alpha]}b_{\alpha\gamma}G_{\gamma\beta}^{[\alpha]}+O_{\prec}(n^{-1}),

so

\displaystyle G_{ij,\alpha\beta}={-}\sum_{\gamma}^{[\alpha]}b_{\alpha\gamma}\mathbf{e}_{i}^{*}(\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}G_{\alpha\alpha})G_{\gamma\beta}^{[\alpha]}\mathbf{e}_{j}+O_{\prec}(n^{-1})

\displaystyle\prec\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\left(\sum_{\gamma}^{[\alpha]}\left|\mathbf{e}_{i}^{*}(\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}G_{\alpha\alpha})G_{\gamma\beta}^{[\alpha]}\mathbf{e}_{j}\right|^{2}\right)^{1/2}+\frac{1}{n}

(116)

\displaystyle\prec\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\,\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}\left(\sum_{\gamma}^{[\alpha]}\left|\mathbf{e}_{i}^{*}G_{\alpha\alpha}G_{\gamma\beta}^{[\alpha]}\mathbf{e}_{j}\right|^{2}\right)^{1/2}+\frac{1}{n},

the first inequality applying independence of
(b_{\alpha\gamma})_{\gamma=1}^{n}

with
(\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}G_{\alpha\alpha})G_{\gamma\beta}^{[\alpha]}
and
the scalar version of Lemma
3.4
(a), and the
second line applying Jensen’s inequality and convexity of the
\ell_{2}
-norm.

Fixing
i\neq j
and
\alpha\neq\beta
,
define
\Delta\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
as the matrix with entries

(117)

\Delta_{\gamma\nu}=\mathbf{e}_{i}^{*}G_{\alpha\nu}G_{\gamma\beta}\mathbf{e}_{j}=\sum_{k}G_{ik,\alpha\nu}G_{kj,\gamma\beta}=\sum_{k}\underbrace{\mathbf{e}_{\gamma}^{*}G_{kj}\mathbf{e}_{\beta}\mathbf{e}_{\alpha}^{*}G_{ik}\mathbf{e}_{\nu}}_{=\Delta_{\gamma\nu}^{k}}.

We claim that (uniformly over
i\neq j
and
\alpha\neq\beta
)

(118)

\|\Delta\|_{\mathrm{op}}\prec n^{-1/2}.

Then, for every
\gamma\neq\alpha
(including
\gamma=\beta
), applying

G_{\alpha\alpha}G_{\gamma\beta}^{[\alpha]}=G_{\alpha\alpha}G_{\gamma\beta}-G_{\alpha\alpha}G_{\gamma\alpha}G_{\alpha\alpha}^{-1}G_{\alpha\beta},\quad G_{\alpha\alpha},G_{\alpha\alpha}^{-1}\prec 1,\quad G_{\gamma\alpha},G_{\alpha\beta}\prec n^{-1/2},

which follow from Lemma
3.7
(c), (
90
),
and (
46
) of
Theorem
4.2
, we have

\mathbf{e}_{i}^{*}G_{\alpha\alpha}G_{\gamma\beta}^{[\alpha]}\mathbf{e}_{j}=\Delta_{\gamma\alpha}-\mathbf{e}_{i}^{*}G_{\alpha\alpha}G_{\gamma\alpha}G_{\alpha\alpha}^{-1}G_{\alpha\beta}\mathbf{e}_{j}=\Delta_{\gamma\alpha}+O_{\prec}(n^{-1})

and hence by (
118
),

\left(\sum_{\gamma}^{[\alpha]}\left|\mathbf{e}_{i}^{*}G_{\alpha\alpha}G_{\gamma\beta}^{[\alpha]}\mathbf{e}_{j}\right|^{2}\right)^{1/2}\leq\|\Delta\|_{\mathrm{op}}+n^{-1/2}\prec n^{-1/2}.

Applying this in (
116
) yields the desired bound

G_{ij,\alpha\beta}\prec n^{-1}
.

It remains to show (
118
). For this, defining

\Delta=\sum_{k}\Delta^{k}
where
\Delta^{k}=G_{kj}E_{\beta\alpha}G_{ik}

as in (
117
), observe that

\|\Delta^{k}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\|G_{kj}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\cdot\|G_{ik}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\prec\begin{cases}n^{-1/2}&\text{ if }k\in\{i,j\}\\
n^{-1}&\text{ if }k\notin\{i,j\}\end{cases}

Then

\Delta=\sum_{k}^{(ij)}(\mathbb{E}_{k}+\mathcal{Q}_{k})[\Delta^{k}]+O_{\prec}(n^{-1/2}).

By Lemma
3.5
(a) applied with the estimates of
Lemma
5.1
, we have

\sum_{k}^{(ij)}\mathcal{Q}_{k}[\Delta^{k}]\prec n^{-1/2}
, so

\Delta=\sum_{k}^{(ij)}\mathbb{E}_{k}[\Delta^{k}]+O_{\prec}(n^{-1/2}).

For any
k\notin\{i,j\}
, we have analogously to (
114
)
and (
115
) that

G_{kk}-M_{k}\prec n^{-1/2},\qquad\sum_{\ell}^{(k)}a_{k\ell}G_{\ell j}^{(k)}\prec n^{-1/2},\qquad\sum_{\ell}^{(k)}G_{i\ell}^{(k)}a_{\ell k}\prec n^{-1/2}.

Hence, applying the resolvent identities of Lemma
3.7
(b),

\displaystyle G_{ik}

\displaystyle={-}\sum_{\ell}^{(k)}G_{i\ell}^{(k)}a_{\ell k}G_{kk}={-}\sum_{\ell}^{(k)}G_{i\ell}^{(k)}a_{\ell k}M_{k}+O_{\prec}(n^{-1})

\displaystyle G_{kj}

\displaystyle={-}\sum_{\ell}^{(k)}G_{kk}a_{k\ell}G_{\ell j}^{(k)}={-}\sum_{\ell}^{(k)}M_{k}a_{k\ell}G_{\ell j}^{(k)}+O_{\prec}(n^{-1}).

Applying this into the definition of
\Delta^{k}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{k}^{(ij)}\mathbb{E}_{k}[\Delta^{k}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}^{(ij)}\mathbb{E}_{k}\Big[G_{kj}E_{\beta\alpha}G_{ik}\Big]

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}^{(ij)}\sum_{\ell,m}^{(k)}\mathbb{E}_{k}[a_{km}a_{\ell k}]M_{k}G_{mj}^{(k)}E_{\beta\alpha}G_{i\ell}^{(k)}M_{k}+O_{\prec}(n^{-1/2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k}^{(ij)}\sum_{\ell}^{(ijk)}M_{k}G_{\ell j}^{(k)}E_{\beta\alpha}G_{i\ell}^{(k)}M_{k}+O_{\prec}(n^{-1/2}),

the last equality using
\mathbb{E}[a_{km}a_{\ell k}]=n^{-1}\mathbf{1}\{\ell=m\}

and then absorbing the summands with
\ell\in\{i,j\}
into the

O_{\prec}(n^{-1/2})
error. Now applying
G_{\ell j}-G_{\ell j}^{(k)},G_{i\ell}-G_{i\ell}^{(k)}\prec n^{-1}

(as follows from Lemma
3.7
(c)),

M_{k}\prec 1
,
and
G_{\ell j}^{(k)},G_{i\ell}^{(k)}\prec n^{-1/2}
for all

\ell\notin\{i,j,k\}
, we get

\displaystyle\sum_{k}^{(ij)}\mathbb{E}_{k}[\Delta^{k}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k}^{(ij)}\sum_{\ell}^{(ijk)}M_{k}G_{\ell j}E_{\beta\alpha}G_{i\ell}M_{k}+O_{\prec}(n^{-1/2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k}\sum_{\ell}M_{k}G_{\ell j}E_{\beta\alpha}G_{i\ell}M_{k}+O_{\prec}(n^{-1/2}),

the second line introducing an additional
O_{\prec}(n^{-1/2})
errors upon
including the summands with
\ell\in\{i,j,k\}
, followed by
k\in\{i,j\}
.
Observing that

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k,\ell=1}^{n}M_{k}G_{\ell j}E_{\beta\alpha}G_{i\ell}M_{k}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}M_{k}\left[\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}G_{\ell j}E_{\beta\alpha}G_{i\ell}\right]M_{k}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}M_{k}\Delta M_{k},

this gives

\Delta=\sum_{k}^{(ij)}\mathbb{E}_{k}[\Delta^{k}]+O_{\prec}(n^{-1/2})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}M_{k}\Delta M_{k}+O_{\prec}(n^{-1/2}).

Thus
\mathcal{L}_{1}(\Delta)\prec n^{-1/2}
where
\mathcal{L}_{1}
is the linear
operator of Lemma
4.5
(in the current setting with
\mathsf{m}_{0}=M_{B}

and
(\mathsf{r}_{0})_{ii}=M_{i}
). By the quantitative invertibility
of
\mathcal{L}_{1}
shown in Lemma
4.5
, this implies the claim
(
118
),
completing the proof of Theorem
2.4
.
∎

6.
Analysis of least-squares problem

In this section, we prove Theorem
2.5
,
Corollary
2.6
, and Proposition
2.7
.
Recall the optimization objective from Section
2
,

f(X)=\frac{1}{2}\|XA+BX\|_{F}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\xi_{i}\theta_{j}x_{ij}^{2}

and its minimizer under a linear constraint,

(119)

\widehat{X}=\argmin_{X\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}}f(X)\text{ subject to }\frac{1}{n}\mathbf{v}^{*}X\mathbf{u}=1.

Proof of Theorem
2.5
.

Consider the following vectorization of (
119
)
(where we use the convention of vectorization by column, i.e.
\mathbf{x}=\sum_{k}\mathbf{e}_{k}\otimes X\mathbf{e}_{k}
)

\displaystyle f(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}^{*}[(A\otimes I+I\otimes B)^{2}+(\Theta\otimes\Xi)]\mathbf{x},

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbf{x}}

\displaystyle=\argmin_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n^{2}}}f(\mathbf{x})\text{ subject to }n^{-1}(\mathbf{u}\otimes\mathbf{v})^{*}\mathbf{x}=1.

Denote

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle=[(A\otimes I+I\otimes B)^{2}+(\Theta\otimes\Xi)]^{-1}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{n\times n},

\displaystyle\mathsf{p}

\displaystyle=[(\mathsf{a}\otimes 1+1\otimes\mathsf{b})^{2}+(\Theta\otimes\Xi)]^{-1}\in\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A},

\displaystyle P_{0}

\displaystyle=(\tau^{\mathcal{D}}\otimes\tau^{\mathcal{D}})[\mathsf{p}].

Simple calculus yields the explicit forms for
\hat{\mathbf{x}}
and
f(\hat{\mathbf{x}})
as

(120)

\hat{\mathbf{x}}=\frac{1}{n^{-2}(\mathbf{u}\otimes\mathbf{v})^{*}P(\mathbf{u}\otimes\mathbf{v})}\cdot n^{-1}P(\mathbf{u}\otimes\mathbf{v}),\qquad f(\hat{\mathbf{x}})=\frac{1}{2}\frac{1}{n^{-2}(\mathbf{u}\otimes\mathbf{v})^{*}P(\mathbf{u}\otimes\mathbf{v})}.

Consider the linearization of
P
given by

\displaystyle\widetilde{P}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}-i\,\Theta\otimes\Xi&A\otimes I+I\otimes B\\
A\otimes I+I\otimes B&-i\,I\otimes I\end{bmatrix}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{bmatrix}\otimes(A\otimes I+I\otimes B)-i\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}\otimes\Theta\otimes\Xi-i\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}\otimes I\otimes I\right)^{-1}.

Denote
\mathbf{e}_{1}=(1,0)\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
. Then by Schur’s complement,

(121)

-i\widetilde{P}=\begin{bmatrix}\Theta\otimes\Xi&i(A\otimes I+I\otimes B)\\
i(A\otimes I+I\otimes B)&I\otimes I\end{bmatrix}^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}[(A\otimes I+I\otimes B)^{2}+\Theta\otimes\Xi]^{-1}&*\\
*&*\end{bmatrix}

so that
P=(\mathbf{e}_{1}\otimes I\otimes I)^{*}[-i\widetilde{P}](\mathbf{e}_{1}\otimes I\otimes I)
. Defining also

(122)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathsf{p}}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{bmatrix}\otimes(\mathsf{a}\otimes 1+1\otimes\mathsf{b})-i\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}\otimes\Theta\otimes\Xi-i\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}\otimes 1\otimes 1\right)^{-1},

\displaystyle\widetilde{P}_{0}

\displaystyle=(I_{2\times 2}\otimes\tau^{\mathcal{D}}\otimes\tau^{\mathcal{D}})[\tilde{\mathsf{p}}],

we have similarly
\mathsf{p}=(\mathbf{e}_{1}\otimes 1\otimes 1)^{*}[-i\tilde{\mathsf{p}}](\mathbf{e}_{1}\otimes 1\otimes 1)
. Then, by (
111
) applied with

(T,T^{\prime})=(\mathbf{e}_{1}^{*}[\cdot]\mathbf{e}_{1},\tau^{\mathcal{D}}\otimes\tau^{\mathcal{D}})
, we have also

P_{0}=(\mathbf{e}_{1}\otimes 1\otimes 1)^{*}[-i\widetilde{P}_{0}](\mathbf{e}_{1}\otimes 1\otimes 1).

By (
47
) of
Theorem
4.2
, uniformly over
\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{u}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with

\|\mathbf{u}\|_{2},\|\mathbf{v}\|_{2},\|\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\|_{2},\|\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\|_{2}\leq\upsilon\sqrt{n}
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{2}}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbf{v}^{\prime})^{*}P(\mathbf{u}\otimes\mathbf{v})

\displaystyle={-i}\left(\mathbf{e}_{1}\otimes\frac{\mathbf{u}^{\prime}}{\sqrt{n}}\otimes\frac{\mathbf{v}^{\prime}}{\sqrt{n}}\right)^{*}\widetilde{P}\left(\mathbf{e}_{1}\otimes\frac{\mathbf{u}}{\sqrt{n}}\otimes\frac{\mathbf{v}}{\sqrt{n}}\right)

\displaystyle={-i}\left(\mathbf{e}_{1}\otimes\frac{\mathbf{u}^{\prime}}{\sqrt{n}}\otimes\frac{\mathbf{v}^{\prime}}{\sqrt{n}}\right)^{*}\widetilde{P}_{0}\left(\mathbf{e}_{1}\otimes\frac{\mathbf{u}}{\sqrt{n}}\otimes\frac{\mathbf{v}}{\sqrt{n}}\right)+O_{\prec}(n^{-1/2})

(123)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{2}}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbf{v}^{\prime})^{*}P_{0}(\mathbf{u}\otimes\mathbf{v})+O_{\prec}(n^{-1/2}).

Here, in the second line, we have applied (
47
) of
Theorem
4.2
twice as in the proof of Theorem
2.4
in Section
5
, first to the second tensor factor
conditional on
B
and the event
\mathcal{E}=\{\|B\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 3\}
with

\mathcal{X}=\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
(the product of
first and third factors),

H=A,\quad\mathsf{x}=\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{bmatrix}\otimes I,\quad\mathsf{z}=i\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}\otimes\Theta\otimes\Xi+i\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}\otimes I\otimes I-\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}\otimes I\otimes B,

and then to the third tensor factor with
\mathcal{X}=\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2}\otimes\mathcal{A}

(the product of the first and second factors),

H=B,\quad\mathsf{x}=\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{bmatrix}\otimes 1,\quad\mathsf{z}=i\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}\otimes\Theta\otimes\Xi+i\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}\otimes 1\otimes I-\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}\otimes\mathsf{a}\otimes I.

Since
\mathsf{p}
is a positive operator, satisfying

\mathsf{p}\geq\|\mathsf{p}^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{-1}(1_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes 1_{\mathcal{A}})
, it follows from positivity
of
\tau^{\mathcal{D}}\otimes\tau^{\mathcal{D}}
(Lemma
D.1
) and the
bounds
\|\mathsf{a}\|_{\mathrm{op}}=\|\mathsf{b}\|_{\mathrm{op}}=2
and
\|\Theta\|_{\mathrm{op}},\|\Xi\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\upsilon

that

P_{0}=\tau^{\mathcal{D}}\otimes\tau^{\mathcal{D}}[\mathsf{p}]\geq\|\mathsf{p}^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{-1}\geq(16+\upsilon^{2})^{-1}.

Therefore, for
\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{u}^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{v}^{\prime}
satisfying
\|\mathbf{u}\|_{2},\|\mathbf{v}\|_{2}\geq\upsilon^{-1}\sqrt{n}
, we have the constant lower bound

\frac{1}{n^{2}}(\mathbf{u}\otimes\mathbf{v})^{*}P_{0}(\mathbf{u}\otimes\mathbf{v})\geq(16+\upsilon^{2})^{-1}\frac{1}{n^{2}}\|\mathbf{u}\|_{2}^{2}\|\mathbf{v}\|_{2}^{2}\geq(16\upsilon^{4}+\upsilon^{6})^{-1},

so we may apply the approximation (
123
) to both the numerator
and denominator of (
120
) to get

\displaystyle f(\hat{\mathbf{x}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\frac{1}{n^{-2}(\mathbf{u}\otimes\mathbf{v})^{*}P_{0}(\mathbf{u}\otimes\mathbf{v})}+O_{\prec}(n^{-1/2}),

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbf{v}^{\prime})^{*}\hat{\mathbf{x}}

\displaystyle=\frac{n^{-2}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbf{v}^{\prime})^{*}P_{0}(\mathbf{u}\otimes\mathbf{v})}{n^{-2}(\mathbf{u}\otimes\mathbf{v})^{*}P_{0}(\mathbf{u}\otimes\mathbf{v})}+O_{\prec}(n^{-1/2}).

Finally, since
P_{0}\in\mathcal{D}\otimes\mathcal{D}\subset\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
is a diagonal matrix, writing
\mathsf{u}=\operatorname{diag}(\mathbf{u})
etc. we have

\displaystyle n^{-2}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbf{v}^{\prime})^{*}P_{0}(\mathbf{u}\otimes\mathbf{v})

\displaystyle=n^{-2}(\operatorname{Tr}\otimes\operatorname{Tr})[(\mathsf{u}^{\prime}\otimes\mathsf{v}^{\prime})^{*}P_{0}(\mathsf{u}\otimes\mathsf{v})]

\displaystyle=(n^{-1}\operatorname{Tr}\otimes n^{-1}\operatorname{Tr})(\tau^{\mathcal{D}}\otimes\tau^{\mathcal{D}})[(\mathsf{u}^{\prime}\otimes\mathsf{v}^{\prime})^{*}\mathsf{p}(\mathsf{u}\otimes\mathsf{v})]

\displaystyle=(\tau\otimes\tau)[(\mathsf{u}^{\prime}\otimes\mathsf{v}^{\prime})^{*}\mathsf{p}(\mathsf{u}\otimes\mathsf{v})].

Applying this identity to both the numerators and denominators above concludes
the proof.
∎

Proof of Corollary
2.6
.

Under the given assumptions, for any fixed
non-commutative polynomial
p
, we have

\lim_{n\to\infty}(\tau\otimes\tau)\Big(p(\mathsf{a}\otimes 1,1\otimes\mathsf{b},\Theta\otimes\Xi,\mathsf{u}\otimes\mathsf{v},\mathsf{u}^{\prime}\otimes\mathsf{v}^{\prime})\Big)=(\tau\otimes\tau)\Big(p(\mathsf{a}\otimes 1,1\otimes\mathsf{b},\theta\otimes\xi,\mathsf{U}\otimes\mathsf{U},\mathsf{U}^{\prime}\otimes\mathsf{U}^{\prime})\Big)

where
\Theta,\mathsf{u},\mathsf{u}^{\prime},\Xi,\mathsf{v},\mathsf{v}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}\subset\mathcal{A}
on the left are real
diagonal matrices, and
\theta,\mathsf{U},\mathsf{U}^{\prime},\xi,\mathsf{V},\mathsf{V}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}
on the right are
commuting, self-adjoint limiting operators, free of
(\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b})
and
for which
(\theta,\mathsf{U},\mathsf{U}^{\prime})
and
(\xi,\mathsf{V},\mathsf{V}^{\prime})

have joint laws under
\tau
given by
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{Q}
, respectively.
The assumptions
\|\Theta\|_{\mathrm{op}},\|\Xi\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\upsilon
and
\Theta,\Xi\geq\delta
imply that
the spectrum of
(\mathsf{a}\otimes 1+1\otimes\mathsf{b})^{2}+\Theta\otimes\Xi

is contained in
[\delta^{2},16+\upsilon^{2}]
. The inverse function

x\mapsto x^{-1}
may be approximated uniformly by polynomials on this
interval, so the above convergence implies

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}(\tau\otimes\tau)[(\mathsf{u}^{\prime}\otimes\mathsf{v}^{\prime})[(\mathsf{a}\otimes 1+1\otimes\mathsf{b})^{2}+\Theta\otimes\Xi]^{-1}(\mathsf{u}\otimes\mathsf{v})]

\displaystyle=(\tau\otimes\tau)[(\mathsf{U}^{\prime}\otimes\mathsf{V}^{\prime})[(\mathsf{a}\otimes 1+1\otimes\mathsf{b})^{2}+\theta\otimes\xi]^{-1}(\mathsf{U}\otimes\mathsf{V})]

where this limit depends only on the joint laws
\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q}
.
Defining this limit quantity as
T^{\prime}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})
, and the analogous limit with

\mathsf{u}=\mathsf{u}^{\prime}
and
\mathsf{v}=\mathsf{v}^{\prime}
as
T(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})
,
the corollary then follows from Theorem
2.5
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
2.7
.

Following the above proof of Corollary
2.6
,
we write
\theta,\mathsf{U},\mathsf{U}^{\prime},\xi,\mathsf{V},\mathsf{V}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}
for the limiting self-adjoint
operators, which are free of
(\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b})
and such
that
(\theta,\mathsf{U},\mathsf{U}^{\prime})
and
(\xi,\mathsf{V},\mathsf{V}^{\prime})

have joint laws under
\tau
given by
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{Q}
.
Thus
\|\mathsf{U}\|_{\infty}\equiv\|\mathsf{U}\|_{\mathrm{op}}
in the statement of
Proposition
2.7
. In this proof, we denote
by
\mathcal{D}\subset\mathcal{A}
the von Neumann subalgebra generated by

\theta,\mathsf{U},\mathsf{U}^{\prime},\xi,\mathsf{V},\mathsf{V}^{\prime}
.

We set
\eta=\min\{\sqrt{x_{a}x_{b}}:x_{a}\in\mathrm{supp}(\theta),\,x_{b}\in\mathrm{supp}(\xi)\}

and define a limiting linearized operator
\tilde{\mathsf{p}}
analogous to
(
122
),

\tilde{\mathsf{p}}=\begin{bmatrix}-i\eta^{-1}\,\theta\otimes\xi&\mathsf{a}\otimes 1+1\otimes\mathsf{b}\\
\mathsf{a}\otimes 1+1\otimes\mathsf{b}&-i\eta\,1\otimes 1\end{bmatrix}^{-1}.

It is direct to check as in (
121
) that we have

(124)

[(\mathsf{a}\otimes 1+1\otimes\mathsf{b})^{2}+\theta\otimes\xi]^{-1}=(\mathbf{e}_{1}\otimes 1\otimes 1)^{*}[-i\eta^{-1}\tilde{\mathsf{p}}](\mathbf{e}_{1}\otimes 1\otimes 1).

Introducing the shorthands

\mathsf{d}_{a}=\theta^{-1/2},\qquad\mathsf{d}_{b}=\xi^{-1/2},

\tilde{\mathsf{d}}=\begin{bmatrix}\eta^{1/2}\mathsf{d}_{a}\otimes\mathsf{d}_{b}&0\\
0&\eta^{-1/2}1\otimes 1\end{bmatrix},\qquad\tilde{\mathsf{a}}=\begin{bmatrix}0&\mathsf{d}_{a}\mathsf{a}\otimes\mathsf{d}_{b}\\
0&0\end{bmatrix},\qquad\tilde{\mathsf{b}}=\begin{bmatrix}0&\mathsf{d}_{a}\otimes\mathsf{d}_{b}\mathsf{b}\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}

and fixing a real argument
z>1
, we write this as

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathsf{p}}

\displaystyle=\left(\tilde{\mathsf{d}}^{-1}\begin{bmatrix}-i\,1\otimes 1&\mathsf{d}_{a}\mathsf{a}\otimes\mathsf{d}_{b}+\mathsf{d}_{a}\otimes\mathsf{d}_{b}\mathsf{b}\\
\mathsf{a}\mathsf{d}_{a}\otimes\mathsf{d}_{b}+\mathsf{d}_{a}\otimes\mathsf{b}\mathsf{d}_{b}&-i\,1\otimes 1\end{bmatrix}\tilde{\mathsf{d}}^{-1}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mathsf{d}}\Big(\underbrace{\tilde{\mathsf{a}}+\tilde{\mathsf{a}}^{*}+\tilde{\mathsf{b}}+\tilde{\mathsf{b}}^{*}}_{=\tilde{\mathsf{q}}}+\,i(z-1)-iz\Big)^{-1}\tilde{\mathsf{d}}.

Note that

\|\mathsf{d}_{a}\mathsf{a}\otimes\mathsf{d}_{b}+\mathsf{d}_{a}\otimes\mathsf{d}_{b}\mathsf{b}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\|\mathsf{d}_{a}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|\mathsf{d}_{b}\|_{\mathrm{op}}(\|\mathsf{a}\|_{\mathrm{op}}+\|\mathsf{b}\|_{\mathrm{op}})\leq 4\eta^{-1},

so
\tilde{\mathsf{q}}=\tilde{\mathsf{a}}+\tilde{\mathsf{a}}^{*}+\tilde{\mathsf{b}}+\tilde{\mathsf{b}}^{*}
is self-adjoint with
spectrum contained in
[-4\eta^{-1},4\eta^{-1}]
. Hence

(125)

\|\tilde{\mathsf{q}}+i(z-1)\|_{\mathrm{op}}=\max\{|\lambda+i(z-1)|:\lambda\in\mathrm{spec}(\tilde{\mathsf{q}})\}\leq\sqrt{(z-1)^{2}+16\eta^{-2}}.

Applying iteratively
(\tilde{\mathsf{q}}+i(z-1)-iz)^{-1}={-}(iz)^{-1}+(iz)^{-1}[\tilde{\mathsf{q}}+i(z-1)](\tilde{\mathsf{q}}+i(z-1)-iz)^{-1}
to
write a series expansion of
(\tilde{\mathsf{q}}+i(z-1)-iz)^{-1}
, we have

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathsf{p}}

\displaystyle={-}\sum_{k=0}^{M-1}(iz)^{-(k+1)}\tilde{\mathsf{d}}[\tilde{\mathsf{q}}+i(z-1)]^{k}\tilde{\mathsf{d}}+\mathsf{r}_{M}

\displaystyle={-}\sum_{k=0}^{M-1}(iz)^{-(k+1)}\sum_{m=0}^{k}\binom{k}{m}[i(z-1)]^{k-m}\tilde{\mathsf{d}}\tilde{\mathsf{q}}^{m}\tilde{\mathsf{d}}+\mathsf{r}_{M}

\displaystyle=i\sum_{m=0}^{M-1}\underbrace{\left(\frac{1}{i^{m}z(z-1)^{m}}\sum_{k=m}^{M-1}\binom{k}{m}\left(\frac{z-1}{z}\right)^{k}\right)}_{=C_{m}(z)}\tilde{\mathsf{d}}\tilde{\mathsf{q}}^{m}\tilde{\mathsf{d}}+\mathsf{r}_{M}

with remainder

\displaystyle\|\mathsf{r}_{M}\|_{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle=\left\|z^{-M}\tilde{\mathsf{d}}[\tilde{\mathsf{q}}+i(z-1)]^{M}(\tilde{\mathsf{q}}-i)^{-1}\tilde{\mathsf{d}}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\eta^{-1}\left(\frac{\sqrt{(z-1)^{2}+16\eta^{-2}}}{z}\right)^{M}.

Here, we have applied (
125
),
\|(\tilde{\mathsf{q}}-i)^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 1
, and
\|\tilde{\mathsf{d}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\eta^{-1/2}
as follows from its
definition. Then, recalling the Schur complement identity (
124
)
and applying also norm contractivity of
\tau\otimes\tau

(Lemma
D.1
), we obtain

\displaystyle(\tau\otimes\tau)[(\mathsf{U}^{\prime}\otimes\mathsf{V}^{\prime})[(\mathsf{a}\otimes 1+1\otimes\mathsf{b})^{2}+\theta\otimes\xi]^{-1}(\mathsf{U}\otimes\mathsf{V})]

(126)

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=0}^{M-1}C_{m}(z)(\tau\otimes\tau)\Big[(\mathbf{e}_{1}\otimes\mathsf{U}^{\prime}\otimes\mathsf{V}^{\prime})^{*}[\eta^{-1}\tilde{\mathsf{d}}\tilde{\mathsf{q}}^{m}\tilde{\mathsf{d}}](\mathbf{e}_{1}\otimes\mathsf{U}\otimes\mathsf{V})\Big]+r_{M}

where
r_{M}
is an error satisfying (
35
).
We remark that the left side is real because all elements are self-adjoint,
while each summand on the right
is real for even
m
and pure imaginary for odd
m
by the definition of

C_{m}(z)
. Thus, taking real parts,
this identity also holds with the summation restricted to even
m
.

We now analyze the summand for each even
m\in\{0,\ldots,M-1\}
.
Let
\mathcal{W}_{m}
be the
set of words in the letters
(\mathsf{A},\mathsf{A}^{*},\mathsf{B},\mathsf{B}^{*})
starting with a letter in

\{\mathsf{A},\mathsf{B}\}
and alternating between a letter
\{\mathsf{A},\mathsf{B}\}
and a letter

\{\mathsf{A}^{*},\mathsf{B}^{*}\}
, understood as non-commutative monic monomials of four variables.
Then, applying the definitions of
\tilde{\mathsf{d}},\tilde{\mathsf{a}},\tilde{\mathsf{b}}
, we have

\displaystyle(\mathbf{e}_{1}\otimes 1\otimes 1)^{*}\eta^{-1}\tilde{\mathsf{d}}(\tilde{\mathsf{a}}+\tilde{\mathsf{a}}^{*}+\tilde{\mathsf{b}}+\tilde{\mathsf{b}}^{*})^{m}\tilde{\mathsf{d}}(\mathbf{e}_{1}\otimes 1\otimes 1)^{*}

\displaystyle=\sum_{w\in\mathcal{W}_{m}}\mathsf{d}_{a}w(\mathsf{d}_{a}\mathsf{a},\mathsf{a}\mathsf{d}_{a},\mathsf{d}_{a},\mathsf{d}_{a})\mathsf{d}_{a}\otimes\mathsf{d}_{b}w(\mathsf{d}_{b},\mathsf{d}_{b},\mathsf{d}_{b}\mathsf{b},\mathsf{b}\mathsf{d}_{b})\mathsf{d}_{b}\in\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A}.

Thus

\displaystyle(\tau\otimes\tau)\Big[(\mathbf{e}_{1}\otimes\mathsf{U}^{\prime}\otimes\mathsf{V}^{\prime})^{*}[\eta^{-1}\tilde{\mathsf{d}}\tilde{\mathsf{q}}^{m}\tilde{\mathsf{d}}](\mathbf{e}_{1}\otimes\mathsf{U}\otimes\mathsf{V})\Big]

\displaystyle=\sum_{w\in\mathcal{W}_{m}}\tau\Big(\mathsf{U}^{\prime}\mathsf{d}_{a}w(\mathsf{d}_{a}\mathsf{a},\mathsf{a}\mathsf{d}_{a},\mathsf{d}_{a},\mathsf{d}_{a})\mathsf{d}_{a}\mathsf{U}\Big)\cdot\tau\Big(\mathsf{V}^{\prime}\mathsf{d}_{b}w(\mathsf{d}_{b},\mathsf{d}_{b},\mathsf{d}_{b}\mathsf{b},\mathsf{b}\mathsf{d}_{b})\mathsf{d}_{b}\mathsf{V}\Big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{w\in\mathcal{W}_{m}}\tau\Big(\mathsf{d}_{a}\mathsf{U}\mathsf{U}^{\prime}\mathsf{d}_{a}w(\mathsf{d}_{a}\mathsf{a},\mathsf{a}\mathsf{d}_{a},\mathsf{d}_{a},\mathsf{d}_{a})\Big)\cdot\tau\Big(\mathsf{d}_{b}\mathsf{V}\mathsf{V}^{\prime}\mathsf{d}_{b}w(\mathsf{d}_{b},\mathsf{d}_{b},\mathsf{d}_{b}\mathsf{b},\mathsf{b}\mathsf{d}_{b})\Big).

For any
\mathsf{d}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{d}_{k+1}\in\mathcal{D}
, by the free moment-cumulant relations

[
52
, Proposition 11.4]
, we have

\tau(\mathsf{d}_{1}\mathsf{a}\mathsf{d}_{2}\mathsf{a}\ldots\mathsf{d}_{k}\mathsf{a}\mathsf{d}_{k+1})=\sum_{\pi\in\operatorname{NC}(2k+1)}\kappa_{\pi}(\mathsf{d}_{1},\mathsf{a},\mathsf{d}_{2},\mathsf{a},\ldots,\mathsf{d}_{k},\mathsf{a},\mathsf{d}_{k+1})

where
\operatorname{NC}(2k+1)
is the set of non-crossing partitions of
(1,2,\ldots,2k+1)

and
\kappa_{\pi}
is the free cumulant associated to each
\pi\in\operatorname{NC}(2k+1)
.
Since
\mathsf{a}
is free of
\mathcal{D}
and has
2^{\text{nd}}
free cumulant equal to 1
and remaining free cumulants 0
[
52
, Example 11.21]
, we have

\kappa_{\pi}(\mathsf{d}_{1},\mathsf{a},\mathsf{d}_{2},\mathsf{a},\ldots,\mathsf{d}_{k},\mathsf{a},\mathsf{d}_{k+1})=0
unless the blocks of

\pi
containing
\mathsf{a}
constitute a non-crossing pairing and are
disjoint from those containing

(\mathsf{d}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{d}_{k+1})
. Let
\operatorname{NC}_{2}(k)
denote the set of all
non-crossing pairings of
\{2,4,6,\ldots,2k\}
corresponding to the locations of
the
\mathsf{a}
’s, and for each
\rho\in\operatorname{NC}_{2}(k)
, let
K(\rho)\in\operatorname{NC}(k+1)
be
its complement in
\{1,2,\ldots,2k+1\}
, i.e. the
coarsest non-crossing partition of the remaining elements

\{1,3,5,\ldots,2k+1\}
for which

\rho\cup K(\rho)
forms a non-crossing partition of
\{1,2,\ldots,2k+1\}
.
Then each
\pi\in\operatorname{NC}(2k+1)
for which

\kappa_{\pi}(\mathsf{d}_{1},\mathsf{a},\mathsf{d}_{2},\mathsf{a},\ldots,\mathsf{d}_{k},\mathsf{a},\mathsf{d}_{k+1})\neq 0

is the union of some
\rho\in\operatorname{NC}_{2}(k)
and some

\bar{\rho}\leq K(\rho)
that refines
K(\rho)
, so we have

\displaystyle\tau(\mathsf{d}_{1}\mathsf{a}\mathsf{d}_{2}\mathsf{a}\ldots\mathsf{d}_{k}\mathsf{a}\mathsf{d}_{k+1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\rho\in\operatorname{NC}_{2}(k)}\sum_{\bar{\rho}\in\operatorname{NC}(k+1):\bar{\rho}\leq K(\rho)}\kappa_{\rho}(\mathsf{a},\ldots,\mathsf{a})\kappa_{\bar{\rho}}(\mathsf{d}_{1},\mathsf{d}_{2},\ldots,\mathsf{d}_{k+1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\rho\in\operatorname{NC}_{2}(k)}\sum_{\bar{\rho}\leq K(\rho)}\kappa_{\bar{\rho}}(\mathsf{d}_{1},\mathsf{d}_{2},\ldots,\mathsf{d}_{k+1})=\sum_{\rho\in\operatorname{NC}_{2}(k)}\prod_{S\in K(\rho)}\tau\left(\prod_{i\in S}\mathsf{d}_{i}\right),

the last equality applying that
\{\bar{\rho}:\bar{\rho}\leq K(\rho)\}
is a
product of non-crossing partitions of the blocks of
K(\rho)
and applying the
free moment-cumulant relation over each block of
K(\rho)
.

Applying this above,
corresponding to each word
w\in\mathcal{W}_{m}
, let
\operatorname{NC}_{w,2}(\mathsf{A})
be the set of all
non-crossing pairings of the letters
\{A,A^{*}\}
of
w
(not necessarily
pairing
A
with
A^{*}
), and let
\operatorname{NC}_{w,2}(\mathsf{B})
be those of the letters

\{B,B^{*}\}
of
w
. Then

\tau\Big(\mathsf{d}_{a}\mathsf{U}\mathsf{U}^{\prime}\mathsf{d}_{a}w(\mathsf{d}_{a}\mathsf{a},\mathsf{a}\mathsf{d}_{a},\mathsf{d}_{a},\mathsf{d}_{a})\Big)\cdot\tau\Big(\mathsf{d}_{b}\mathsf{V}\mathsf{V}^{\prime}\mathsf{d}_{b}w(\mathsf{d}_{b},\mathsf{d}_{b},\mathsf{d}_{b}\mathsf{b},\mathsf{b}\mathsf{d}_{b})\Big)\\
=\sum_{\rho_{a}\in\operatorname{NC}_{w,2}(\mathsf{A})}\sum_{\rho_{b}\in\operatorname{NC}_{w,2}(\mathsf{B})}\mathrm{val}(\rho_{a},\rho_{b})

where
\mathrm{val}(\cdot)
is precisely the quantity defined in
Section
2.2.1
.
Finally, we observe that summing first over
words
w\in\mathcal{W}_{m}
of
(A,A^{*},B,B^{*})
and then over pairings
\rho_{a}\in\operatorname{NC}_{w,2}(\mathsf{A})
and
\rho_{b}\in\operatorname{NC}_{w,2}(\mathsf{B})
is equivalent to
summing over all disjoint non-crossing pairings
(\rho_{a},\rho_{b})\in\operatorname{NC}_{2,2}(m)
, and then identifying
w
as the word with letters

\{\mathsf{A},\mathsf{A}^{*}\}
for elements of
\rho_{a}
and
\{\mathsf{B},\mathsf{B}^{*}\}
for elements of

\rho_{b}
and that alternates between
\{A,B\}
and
\{A^{*},B^{*}\}
. Thus

(\tau\otimes\tau)\Big[(\mathbf{e}_{1}\otimes\mathsf{U}^{\prime}\otimes\mathsf{V}^{\prime})^{*}[\eta^{-1}\tilde{\mathsf{d}}\tilde{\mathsf{q}}^{m}\tilde{\mathsf{d}}](\mathbf{e}_{1}\otimes\mathsf{U}\otimes\mathsf{V})\Big]=\sum_{(\rho_{a},\rho_{b})\in\operatorname{NC}_{2,2}(m)}\mathrm{val}(\rho_{a},\rho_{b}).

A direct calculation shows that
this identity holds also for
m=0
, upon defining

\operatorname{NC}_{2,2}(0)
to have the single pair
(\emptyset,\emptyset)

with its value defined as in (
34
).
Applying this back to (
126
)
and using
i^{m}=(-1)^{m/2}
in
C_{m}(z)
concludes the proof.
∎

Appendix A
Contour integral representation in the case
\Theta=\Xi=\eta I

The optimization problem (
1
) with
\Theta=\Xi=\eta I

and
\eta\sim 1/(\log n)^{C}
was studied previously in
[
31
]
.
In this setting, its solution is given explicitly by

\widehat{X}=\frac{1}{n^{-2}(\mathbf{u}\otimes\mathbf{v})^{*}P(\mathbf{u}\otimes\mathbf{v})}\cdot n^{-1}P(\mathbf{u}\otimes\mathbf{v})\quad\text{ where }\quad P=[(A\otimes I+I\otimes B)^{2}+\eta^{2}I\otimes I]^{-1}.

Applying the linearization

[(A\otimes I+I\otimes B)^{2}+\eta^{2}I\otimes I]^{-1}=\frac{1}{\eta}\Im[A\otimes I+I\otimes B-i\eta I\otimes I]^{-1},

the analyses of
[
31
]

rested on a deterministic approximation for the resolvent

R(z)=(Q-z\,I\otimes I)^{-1},\qquad Q=A\otimes I+I\otimes B

at spectral scales
\Im z\sim 1/(\log n)^{C}
decaying slowly with
n
.

This setting is special because the matrices
A\otimes I
and
I\otimes B

constituting
Q

commute. In this setting, writing the spectral decompositions
A=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\lambda_{j}\mathbf{u}_{j}\mathbf{u}_{j}^{*}
and
B=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\mu_{k}\mathbf{v}_{k}\mathbf{v}_{k}^{*}
, the spectral
decomposition of
Q
is explicit and given by

Q=\sum_{j,k=1}^{n}(\lambda_{j}+\mu_{k})(\mathbf{u}_{j}\otimes\mathbf{v}_{k})(\mathbf{u}_{j}\otimes\mathbf{v}_{k})^{*}.

In particular, the limit eigenvalue distribution of
Q
is the (classical)
convolution of the semicircle law with itself. Furthermore, defining a contour

\Gamma
enclosing
\{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n}\}
and such that

|\Im w|\leq(\Im z)/2
for all
w\in\Gamma
,
by the Cauchy integral formula applied to

f_{k}(w)=(w+\mu_{k}-z)^{-1}
(which is analytic inside
\Gamma
)
the resolvent of
Q
has the explicit contour integral representation

\displaystyle R(z)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j,k=1}^{n}\frac{1}{\lambda_{j}+\mu_{k}-z}\mathbf{u}_{j}\mathbf{u}_{j}^{*}\otimes\mathbf{v}_{k}\mathbf{v}_{k}^{*}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{\Gamma}\frac{1}{w-\lambda_{j}}\frac{1}{w+\mu_{k}-z}\mathbf{u}_{j}\mathbf{u}_{j}^{*}\otimes\mathbf{v}_{k}\mathbf{v}_{k}^{*}\,dw

\displaystyle={-}\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{\Gamma}R_{A}(w)\otimes R_{B}(z-w)\,dw

where
R_{A}(z)=(A-zI)^{-1}
and
R_{B}(z)=(B-zI)^{-1}
. From this representation,
resolvent estimates of the form in Theorem
2.4
may be
deduced from known local laws for the resolvents of the Wigner matrices
A
and

B
, see e.g.
[
28
,
23
]
,
and analysis of this commutative case suggests that the estimates in
Theorem
2.4
are also optimal (for fixed
z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}
).

We emphasize that this type of analysis and contour integral representation
does not extend to models of the form

Q=A\otimes I+I\otimes B+\Theta\otimes\Xi
when
\Theta\otimes\Xi
does
not commute with either
A\otimes I
or
I\otimes B
, which is the focus of our
current work.

Appendix B
Operator concentration inequalities

In this section, we prove Lemma
3.4
using the following
version of the non-commutative Rosenthal inequality of

[
39
]
. We recall that
\mathcal{X}
is a von Neumann algebra with
faithful, normal, tracial state
\phi
, and
L^{p}(\mathcal{X})
is its associated
non-commutative
L^{p}
space (c.f. Appendix
D
) with norm

\|\mathsf{x}\|_{p}=\phi((\mathsf{x}^{*}\mathsf{x})^{p/2})^{1/p}
.

Lemma B.1

(
[
39
]
, Theorem 2.1)
.

Let
\mathcal{Y}\subset\mathcal{X}
be a von Neumann subalgebra with
\phi
-invariant
conditional expectation (c.f. Lemma
D.1
)

\phi^{\mathcal{Y}}:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
. Suppose
\mathsf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{x}_{n}\in L^{p}(\mathcal{X})
satisfy
\phi^{\mathcal{Y}}(\mathsf{x}_{i})=0
, and are independent over
\mathcal{Y}

in the sense that for each
i
, each
\mathsf{x}
in the von Neumann
subalgebra generated by
\mathsf{x}_{i}
, and each
\mathsf{x}^{\prime}
in the von
Neumann subalgebra generated by
\{\mathsf{x}_{j}:j\neq i\}
, we have

\phi^{\mathcal{Y}}(\mathsf{x}\mathsf{x}^{\prime})=\phi^{\mathcal{Y}}(\mathsf{x})\phi^{\mathcal{Y}}(\mathsf{x}^{\prime})
.

Then for any
p\in[2,\infty)
and a universal constant
C>0
,

\bigg\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{x}_{i}\bigg\|_{p}\leq Cp\max\bigg\{\bigg\|\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\phi^{\mathcal{Y}}(\mathsf{x}_{i}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{*})\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg\|_{p},\;\bigg\|\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\phi^{\mathcal{Y}}(\mathsf{x}_{i}^{*}\mathsf{x}_{i})\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg\|_{p},\;\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|\mathsf{x}_{i}\|_{p}^{p}\bigg)^{1/p}\bigg\}.

Lemma B.2

(Decoupling)
.

Let
(\alpha_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
be a sequence of independent
\mathbb{C}
-valued random
variables, let
(\alpha_{i}^{\prime})_{i=1}^{n}
be an independent
copy of
(\alpha_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
, and let

(\mathsf{y}_{ij}:i,j=1,\ldots,n)
be elements of a Banach space with norm

\|\cdot\|
. Then for a universal constant
C>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg\|\sum_{i\neq j}\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}\mathsf{y}_{ij}\bigg\|^{p}\bigg]\leq C^{p+1}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg\|\sum_{i\neq j}\alpha_{i}\alpha_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{y}_{ij}\bigg\|^{p}\bigg]

Proof.

By
[
15
, Theorem 1]
, for a universal
constant
C>0
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p}\,\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg\|\sum_{i\neq j}\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}\mathsf{y}_{ij}\bigg\|^{p}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{p-1}\mathbb{P}\bigg[\bigg\|\sum_{i\neq j}\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}\mathsf{y}_{ij}\bigg\|\geq t\bigg]\;dt

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}Ct^{p-1}\mathbb{P}\bigg[C\bigg\|\sum_{i\neq j}\alpha_{i}\alpha_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{y}_{ij}\bigg\|\geq t\bigg]\;dt=\frac{C^{p+1}}{p}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg\|\sum_{i\neq j}\alpha_{i}\alpha_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{y}_{ij}\bigg\|^{p}\bigg].

∎

Proof of Lemma
3.4
.

Throughout,
C_{p},C_{p}^{\prime},C_{p}^{\prime\prime}
denote
p
-dependent constants
that may change from instance to instance.

For (a), fix any
p\geq 2
. We apply Lemma
B.1
in the
setting of
[
39
, Example 1.3]
:
Let
L^{\infty}(\Omega)
be the von Neumann algebra of bounded scalar
random variables over the underlying probability space

(\Omega,\mathscr{F},\mathbb{P})
, and consider
\mathcal{M}=L^{\infty}(\Omega)\otimes\mathcal{X}

equipped with the state

\mathbb{E}\circ\phi
. Then
\alpha_{i}\mathsf{x}_{i}\in L^{p}(\mathcal{M},\mathbb{E}\circ\phi)
,

\mathbb{E}:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{X}
coincides with the conditional
expectation onto the subalgebra
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathcal{M}
, and

\{\alpha_{i}\mathsf{x}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
are independent over
\mathcal{X}
in the sense of
Lemma
B.1
, so Lemma
B.1
shows

\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}\mathsf{x}_{i}\bigg\|_{p}^{p}\bigg]\leq C_{p}\max\bigg\{\bigg\|\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}[|\alpha_{i}|^{2}]\mathsf{x}_{i}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{*}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg\|_{p}^{p},\;\bigg\|\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}[|\alpha_{i}|^{2}]\mathsf{x}_{i}^{*}\mathsf{x}_{i}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg\|_{p}^{p},\;\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}[|\alpha_{i}|^{p}]\|\mathsf{x}_{i}\|_{p}^{p}\bigg\}.

Applying the bounds
\mathbb{E}[|\alpha_{i}|^{2}],\mathbb{E}[|\alpha_{i}|^{p}]\leq C_{p}
, operator monotonicity of the square-root

0\leq\mathsf{x}\leq\mathsf{y}\Rightarrow\mathsf{x}^{1/2}\leq\mathsf{y}^{1/2}
, and monotonicity of the

L^{p}
-norm on the positive cone (Lemma
D.2
), this implies

(127)

\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}\mathsf{x}_{i}\bigg\|_{p}^{p}\bigg]\leq C_{p}^{\prime}\max\bigg\{\bigg\|\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{x}_{i}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{*}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg\|_{p}^{p},\;\bigg\|\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{*}\mathsf{x}_{i}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg\|_{p}^{p},\;\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|\mathsf{x}_{i}\|_{p}^{p}\bigg\}.

Here, for
p\geq 2
,
the third term is bounded by the second by the following argument (see
also
[
39
, Eq. (2.4)]
): Consider

\hat{\mathsf{x}}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}E_{i1}\otimes\mathsf{x}_{i}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}\otimes\mathcal{X}

equipped with the trace
n^{-1}\operatorname{Tr}\otimes\phi
,
and the linear map
T:\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}\otimes\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}\otimes\mathcal{X}
defined by

T(E_{ij}\otimes\mathsf{x})=\begin{cases}E_{i,j+i-1}\otimes\mathsf{x}&\text{if $j=1$}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

where
j+i-1
is
interpreted modulo
n
. Thus
\hat{\mathsf{x}}
has
\mathcal{X}
-valued entries

\mathsf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{x}_{n}
along the first column, and
T(\hat{\mathsf{x}})
has these entries
instead along the main diagonal. We have

\displaystyle\|\hat{\mathsf{x}}\|_{p}^{p}

\displaystyle=\|(\hat{\mathsf{x}}^{*}\hat{\mathsf{x}})^{1/2}\|_{p}^{p}=\frac{1}{n}\bigg\|\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{*}\mathsf{x}_{i}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg\|_{p}^{p},

\displaystyle\|T(\hat{\mathsf{x}})\|_{p}^{p}

\displaystyle=\|(T(\hat{\mathsf{x}})^{*}T(\hat{\mathsf{x}}))^{1/2}\|_{p}^{p}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|(\mathsf{x}_{i}^{*}\mathsf{x}_{i})^{1/2}\|_{p}^{p}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|\mathsf{x}_{i}\|_{p}^{p}.

For
p=2
, this shows
\|T(\hat{\mathsf{x}})\|_{2}=\|\hat{\mathsf{x}}\|_{2}
. For
p=\infty
, we have

\|T(\hat{\mathsf{x}})\|_{\mathrm{op}}=\max_{i}\|\mathsf{x}_{i}\|_{\mathrm{op}}=\max_{i}\|(\mathsf{x}_{i}^{*}\mathsf{x}_{i})^{1/2}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\bigg\|\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{*}\mathsf{x}_{i}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg\|_{\mathrm{op}}=\|\hat{\mathsf{x}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}

by operator monotonicity of the square-root and monotonicity of the operator
norm on the positive cone. Then

\|T(\hat{\mathsf{x}})\|_{p}\leq\|\hat{\mathsf{x}}\|_{p}
for all
p\in[2,\infty]

by the Riesz-Thorin interpolation (Lemma
D.4
). Thus, the
third term of (
127
) is at most the first, yielding the
claim of part (a).

Part (b) follows from a two-fold application of part (a): Let
\mathsf{x}_{i}=\sum_{j}\beta_{j}\mathsf{y}_{ij}
, so
\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}\mathsf{x}_{i}=\sum_{i,j}\alpha_{i}\beta_{j}\mathsf{y}_{ij}
.
Then, applying part (a) conditional on
(\beta_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
, we have

(128)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg\|\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}\beta_{j}\mathsf{y}_{ij}\bigg\|_{p}^{p}\bigg]\leq C_{p}\max\bigg\{\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg\|\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{x}_{i}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{*}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg\|_{p}^{p}\bigg],\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg\|\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{*}\mathsf{x}_{i}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg\|_{p}^{p}\bigg]\bigg\}

To apply part (a) again on these errors, define

\hat{\mathsf{y}}_{ij}=E_{1i}\otimes\mathsf{y}_{ij}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}\otimes\mathcal{X}

equipped with the trace
n^{-1}\operatorname{Tr}\otimes\phi
.
It follows that
\hat{\mathsf{y}}_{ij}\hat{\mathsf{y}}_{kl}^{*}=\mathbf{1}_{i=k}E_{11}\otimes\mathsf{y}_{ij}\mathsf{y}_{il}^{*}
, so

E_{11}\otimes\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{x}_{i}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{*}=\sum_{j,l=1}^{n}\beta_{j}\bar{\beta}_{l}\sum_{i=1}^{n}E_{11}\otimes\mathsf{y}_{ij}\mathsf{y}_{il}^{*}=\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\beta_{j}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\hat{\mathsf{y}}_{ij}\bigg)\bigg(\sum_{l=1}^{n}\beta_{l}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\hat{\mathsf{y}}_{kl}\bigg)^{*}.

Then

\bigg\|\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{x}_{i}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{*}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg\|_{p}^{p}=n\bigg\|\bigg(E_{11}\otimes\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{x}_{i}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{*}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg\|_{p}^{p}=n\bigg\|\sum_{j=1}^{n}\beta_{j}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\hat{\mathsf{y}}_{ij}\bigg\|_{p}^{p}.

Applying part (a) with
\hat{\mathsf{x}}_{j}=\sum_{i}\hat{\mathsf{y}}_{ij}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}\otimes\mathcal{X}
in place of
\mathsf{x}_{j}\in\mathcal{X}
, this is bounded as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg\|\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{x}_{i}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{*}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg\|_{p}^{p}\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq C_{p}n\max\bigg\{\bigg\|\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\hat{\mathsf{x}}_{j}\hat{\mathsf{x}}_{j}^{*}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg\|_{p}^{p},\bigg\|\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\hat{\mathsf{x}}_{j}^{*}\hat{\mathsf{x}}_{j}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg\|_{p}^{p}\bigg\}

\displaystyle=C_{p}n\max\bigg\{\bigg\|\bigg(E_{11}\otimes\sum_{i,j}\mathsf{y}_{ij}\mathsf{y}_{ij}^{*}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg\|_{p}^{p},\bigg\|\bigg(\sum_{i,j,k}E_{ik}\otimes\mathsf{y}_{ij}^{*}\mathsf{y}_{kj}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg\|_{p}^{p}\bigg\}.

The first term is
n^{-1}\|(\sum_{i,j}\mathsf{y}_{ij}\mathsf{y}_{ij}^{*})^{1/2}\|_{p}^{p}
.
For the second term,
we identify
\sum_{i,j,k}E_{ik}\otimes\mathsf{y}_{ij}^{*}\mathsf{y}_{kj}={\mathsf{Y}^{\mathsf{t}}}^{*}{\mathsf{Y}^{\mathsf{t}}}
where

\mathsf{Y}^{\mathsf{t}}=\sum_{i,j}E_{ji}\otimes\mathsf{y}_{ij}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}\otimes\mathcal{X}
, and
we apply
\|({\mathsf{Y}^{\mathsf{t}}}^{*}{\mathsf{Y}^{\mathsf{t}}})^{1/2}\|_{p}^{p}=\|\mathsf{Y}^{\mathsf{t}}\|_{p}^{p}
.
We have analogously
\mathbb{E}\|(\sum_{i}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{*}\mathsf{x}_{i})^{1/2}\|_{p}^{p}\leq C_{p}n\max(n^{-1}\|(\sum_{i,j}\mathsf{y}_{ij}^{*}\mathsf{y}_{ij})^{1/2}\|_{p}^{p},\|\mathsf{Y}\|_{p}^{p})
,
and combining these gives part (b).

Finally, part (c) follows from part (b)
and the decoupling result of Lemma
B.2
:
Since
\mathsf{y}_{ii}=0
for all
i=1,\ldots,n
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg\|\sum_{i\neq j}\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}\mathsf{y}_{ij}\bigg\|_{p}^{p}\bigg]\leq C_{p}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg\|\sum_{i\neq j}\alpha_{i}\alpha_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{y}_{ij}\bigg\|_{p}^{p}\bigg]=C_{p}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg\|\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}\alpha_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{y}_{ij}\bigg\|_{p}^{p}\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq C_{p}^{\prime}\max\bigg\{\bigg\|\bigg(\sum_{i\neq j}\mathsf{y}_{ij}\mathsf{y}_{ij}^{*}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg\|_{p}^{p},\bigg\|\bigg(\sum_{i\neq j}\mathsf{y}_{ij}^{*}\mathsf{y}_{ij}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg\|_{p}^{p},n\|\mathsf{Y}\|_{p}^{p},n\|\mathsf{Y}^{\mathsf{t}}\|_{p}^{p}\bigg\}.

∎

Appendix C
Fluctuation averaging

In this section, we prove Lemma
3.5
. The proof is
analogous to the argument in the scalar setting of

[
29
, Lemma A.2]
. For part (c), we
will apply the following
combinatorial lemma from
[
29
]
.

Lemma C.1

(
[
29
]
, Lemma
A.3)
.

Fix
l\geq 1
. For each
a=1,\ldots,l
, let
B^{a}=(B_{ij}^{a})_{i,j=1}^{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
satisfy

\displaystyle B_{ij}^{a}\geq 0,\quad\quad B_{ii}^{a}=0,\quad\quad\|B^{a}\|_{F}\leq 1\qquad\text{ for all }i,j=1,\ldots,n.

For
(\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j})=(i_{1},...,i_{l},j_{1},...,j_{l})\in\{1,\ldots,n\}^{2l}
, let
T(\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j})
be the set of elements of

\{1,\ldots,n\}
that appear exactly once in
(\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j})
. Then for some constant

C_{l}>0
and all
t\in\{0,\ldots,2l\}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{(\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j})\in\{1,\ldots,n\}^{2l}:|T(\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j})|=t}\;\prod_{a=1}^{l}B_{i_{a}j_{a}}^{a}\leq C_{l}n^{t/2}

Proof of Lemma
3.5
.

For part (a), fix any
\epsilon,D>0
and
p\in[1,\infty)
.
Pick an even integer
l>p
such that
\epsilon(l-1)>D+1
. Then by
monotonicity of
\|\cdot\|_{p}
in
p
(Lemma
D.2
),

(129)

\mathbb{E}\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}u_{i}\mathsf{x}_{i}\right\|_{p}^{l}\leq\mathbb{E}\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}u_{i}\mathsf{x}_{i}\right\|_{l}^{l}=\mathbb{E}\phi\left(\left[\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}u_{i}\mathsf{x}_{i}\right)\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bar{u}_{i}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{*}\right)\right]^{l/2}\right)=\sum_{\mathbf{i}}u_{\mathbf{i}}\mathbb{E}\phi\left(\mathsf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}\right)

where we denote

\displaystyle\mathbf{i}=(i_{1},i_{2},...,i_{l}),\quad\sum_{\mathbf{i}}=\sum_{i_{1},...,i_{l}=1}^{n},\quad u_{\mathbf{i}}=u_{i_{1}}\bar{u}_{i_{2}}\cdots u_{i_{l-1}}\bar{u}_{i_{l}},\quad\mathsf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathsf{x}_{i_{1}}\mathsf{x}_{i_{2}}^{*}\cdots\mathsf{x}_{i_{l-1}}\mathsf{x}_{i_{l}}^{*}=\prod_{a=1}^{l}\widetilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i_{a}}

and write
\widetilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i_{a}}=\mathsf{x}_{i_{a}}
if
a
is odd and

\widetilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i_{a}}=\mathsf{x}_{i_{a}}^{*}
if
a
is even.
Here and below, the product over
a=1,\ldots,l
is non-commutative, and should be
understood in the ordered sense.

For fixed
\mathbf{i}
, let

T\equiv T(\mathbf{i})\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}
be the indices that appear exactly
once in
\mathbf{i}=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{l})
. Using that
\{\mathbb{E}_{j},\mathcal{Q}_{j}\}
commute, we have the identity

\displaystyle\mathsf{x}=\left(\prod_{j\in T}(\mathbb{E}_{j}+\mathcal{Q}_{j})\right)[\mathsf{x}]=\sum_{S\subseteq T}\mathbb{E}_{T\setminus S}\mathcal{Q}_{S}[\mathsf{x}].

(In the case
T=\emptyset
, this is the trivial identity
\mathsf{x}=\mathsf{x}
.)
Applying this to each
\widetilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i_{a}}
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}=\sum_{S_{1},\ldots,S_{l}\subseteq T}\mathsf{x}(S_{1},\ldots,S_{l}),\quad\quad\mathsf{x}(S_{1},\ldots,S_{l})=\prod_{a=1}^{l}\mathbb{E}_{T\setminus S_{a}}\mathcal{Q}_{S_{a}}[\widetilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i_{a}}].

By Hölder’s inequality (Lemma
D.2
) and
(
37
),

\displaystyle|\phi(\mathsf{x}(S_{1},\ldots,S_{l}))|\leq\prod_{a=1}^{l}\|\mathbb{E}_{T\setminus S_{a}}\mathcal{Q}_{S_{a}}[\widetilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i_{a}}]\|_{l}\leq\prod_{a=1}^{l}\mathbb{E}_{T\setminus S_{a}}\|\mathcal{Q}_{S_{a}}[\widetilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i_{a}}]\|_{l}.

Let us write
S\setminus i
for
S
removing
i
if
i\in S
, or
for
S
itself if
i\notin S
. Since
\mathbb{E}_{i_{a}}[\widetilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i_{a}}]=0
by
assumption, we have
\mathcal{Q}_{i_{a}}[\widetilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i_{a}}]=\widetilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i_{a}}
, so

\mathcal{Q}_{S_{a}}[\widetilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i_{a}}]=\mathcal{Q}_{S_{a}\setminus i_{a}}[\widetilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i_{a}}]
.
Then the given condition (
39
) and
Lemma
3.2
(c) (in the setting of scalar random variables) imply

\mathbb{E}_{T\setminus S_{a}}\|\mathcal{Q}_{S_{a}}[\widetilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i_{a}}]\|_{l}\prec n^{-\alpha-\beta|S_{a}\setminus i_{a}|}
, for each fixed
l

uniformly over
i_{a}\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
and over
S_{a}\subseteq T\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}
with
|T|\leq l
. Thus, multiplying across
a=1,\ldots,l

and taking the full expectation, for all
n\geq n_{0}(l,\epsilon)
we have

(130)

|\mathbb{E}\phi(\mathsf{x}(S_{1},\ldots,S_{l}))|\leq\mathbb{E}|\phi(\mathsf{x}(S_{1},\ldots,S_{l}))|\leq n^{-\alpha l-\beta\sum_{a=1}^{l}|S_{a}\setminus i_{a}|+\epsilon}.

Now consider any
a\in\{1,\ldots,l\}
such that
i_{a}\in T
. Observe that

•

\mathsf{x}(S_{1},\ldots,S_{l})=0
unless
i_{a}\in S_{a}
. Indeed, if instead

i_{a}\in T\setminus S_{a}
, then the assumption
\mathbb{E}_{i_{a}}[\widetilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i_{a}}]=0
implies
\mathbb{E}_{T\setminus S_{a}}\mathcal{Q}_{S_{a}}[\widetilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i_{a}}]=0
.

•

\mathbb{E}\phi(\mathsf{x}(S_{1},\ldots,S_{l}))=0
unless also
i_{a}\in S_{b}
for some

b\neq a
. Indeed, if instead
i_{a}\in T\setminus S_{b}
for every
b\neq a
,
then
\mathbb{E}_{T\setminus S_{b}}\mathcal{Q}_{S_{b}}[\widetilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i_{b}}]=\mathbb{E}_{i_{a}}\mathbb{E}_{T\setminus(S_{b}\cup\{i_{a}\})}\mathcal{Q}_{S_{b}}[\widetilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i_{b}}]
is
\mathscr{G}_{i_{a}}
-measurable, hence

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{i_{a}}\mathsf{x}(S_{1},\ldots,S_{l})=\prod_{b<a}\mathbb{E}_{T\setminus S_{b}}\mathcal{Q}_{S_{b}}[\widetilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i_{b}}]\cdot\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{i_{a}}\mathbb{E}_{T\setminus S_{a}}\mathcal{Q}_{S_{a}}[\widetilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i_{a}}]}_{=0}\cdot\prod_{b>a}\mathbb{E}_{T\setminus S_{b}}\mathcal{Q}_{S_{b}}[\widetilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i_{b}}]=0

where the middle term is 0 because
\mathbb{E}_{i_{a}}\mathcal{Q}_{S_{a}}=0
for
i_{a}\in S_{a}
.
Then by linearity of
\phi
,

\mathbb{E}\phi(\mathsf{x}(S_{1},\ldots,S_{l}))=\phi(\mathbb{E}\mathsf{x}(S_{1},\ldots,S_{l}))=0
.

Thus, if
\mathbb{E}\phi(\mathsf{x}(S_{1},\ldots,S_{l}))\neq 0
, then each
i_{a}\in T

must appear in both
S_{a}
and
some set
S_{b}
for
b\neq a
, where
i_{a}\neq i_{b}
because
i_{a}

appears only once in
\mathbf{i}=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{l})
by definition of
T
. So

\sum_{a=1}^{l}|S_{a}\setminus i_{a}|\geq|T|
. Applying this to
(
130
) and then to (
129
),
since for each fixed
\mathbf{i}
and
T\equiv T(\mathbf{i})

the number of choices of subsets

S_{1},\ldots,S_{l}\subseteq T
is at most a constant
C_{l}
,

(131)

\mathbb{E}\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}u_{i}\mathsf{x}_{i}\right\|_{p}^{l}\leq C_{l}n^{-\alpha l+\epsilon}\sum_{\mathbf{i}}|u_{\mathbf{i}}|n^{-\beta|\mathcal{T(\mathbf{i})}|}\leq C_{l}n^{-\alpha l+\epsilon}\|\mathbf{u}\|_{\infty}^{l}\sum_{t=1}^{l}n^{-\beta t}\cdot|\{\mathbf{i}:T(\mathbf{i})=t\}|.

By scale invariance of the statement of the lemma,
let us assume without loss of generality that
\|\mathbf{u}\|_{\infty}=n^{-1}
.
Applying this and the bound
|\{\mathbf{i}:T(\mathbf{i})=t\}|\leq C_{l}^{\prime}n^{t+(l-t)/2}
for a
constant
C_{l}^{\prime}>0
, we get (for different constants
C_{l},C_{l}^{\prime}>0
)

\mathbb{E}\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}u_{i}\mathsf{x}_{i}\right\|_{p}^{l}\leq C_{l}n^{-\alpha l+\epsilon}\sum_{t=1}^{l}(n^{-\beta})^{t}(n^{-1/2})^{l-t}\leq C_{l}n^{-\alpha l+\epsilon}\sum_{t=1}^{l}(n^{-\beta}+n^{-1/2})^{l}\leq C_{l}^{\prime}n^{-(\alpha+\beta^{\prime})l+\epsilon}

where
\beta^{\prime}=\min\{1/2,\beta\}
. Then by Markov’s inequality under our choices
l>p
and

\epsilon(l-1)>D+1
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}u_{i}\mathsf{x}_{i}\right\|_{p}\geq n^{-\alpha-\beta^{\prime}+\epsilon}\right)\leq n^{(\alpha+\beta^{\prime}-\epsilon)l}\,\mathbb{E}\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}u_{i}\mathsf{x}_{i}\right\|_{p}^{l}\leq C_{l}^{\prime}n^{-\epsilon(l-1)}<n^{-D}

for all
n\geq n_{0}(l,\epsilon,D)
. Here
l
depends only on
(p,\epsilon,D)
,
showing
\sum_{i}u_{i}\mathsf{x}_{i}\prec n^{-\alpha-\beta^{\prime}}
as claimed in part (a).

The argument for part (b) is the same, until the analysis of
(
131
) where we apply a different
counting argument: By scale invariance, we may consider
without loss of generality
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
with
\|\mathbf{u}\|_{2}=1
. Under the given
condition (
40
), specializing to
\beta=1/2
,
the first inequality of (
131
) becomes

\mathbb{E}\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}u_{i}\mathsf{x}_{i}\right\|_{p}^{l}\leq C_{l}n^{-\alpha l+\epsilon}\sum_{\mathbf{i}}|u_{\mathbf{i}}|n^{-|\mathcal{T(\mathbf{i})}|/2}.

Now let
\pi(\mathbf{i})
be the partition of
\{1,\ldots,l\}
induced by coincidence of
indices in
\mathbf{i}
, i.e.
a,b
belong to the same block of
\pi(\mathbf{i})
if and only
if
i_{a}=i_{b}
. Then
|T(\mathbf{i})|\equiv|T(\pi(\mathbf{i}))|
is the number of singleton
blocks of
\pi(\mathbf{i})
, depending on
\mathbf{i}
only via
\pi(\mathbf{i})
, so
we may write the above as

(132)

\mathbb{E}\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}u_{i}\mathsf{x}_{i}\right\|_{p}^{l}\leq C_{l}n^{-\alpha l+\epsilon}\sum_{\pi}n^{-|T(\pi)|/2}\sum_{\mathbf{i}:\pi(\mathbf{i})=\pi}|u_{\mathbf{i}}|.

Here
\sum_{\mathbf{i}:\pi(\mathbf{i})=\pi}|u_{\mathbf{i}}|
is a sum over one
(distinct) index
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
for each block
P
of
\pi
, for which
we have
\sum_{\mathbf{i}:\pi(\mathbf{i})=\pi}|u_{\mathbf{i}}|\leq\prod_{P\in\pi}\sum_{i=1}^{n}|u_{i}|^{|P|}
. Under our assumed normalization
\|\mathbf{u}\|_{2}=1
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}|u_{i}|\leq\sqrt{n},\quad\quad\sum_{i=1}^{n}|u_{i}|^{k}\leq\|\mathbf{u}\|_{\infty}^{k-2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}|u_{i}|^{2}\leq 1\quad\text{ for any }k\geq 2.

Thus
\sum_{\mathbf{i}:\pi(\mathbf{i})=\pi}|u_{\mathbf{i}}|\leq n^{|T(\pi)|/2}
. Applying this to
(
132
), we have

\mathbb{E}\|\sum_{i}u_{i}\mathsf{x}_{i}\|_{p}^{l}\leq C_{l}^{\prime}n^{-\alpha l+\epsilon}
. The proof of (b) now follows
by the same Markov inequality argument as in part (a).

Part (c) is also similar: By scale invariance, we may consider
U\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
such that

\sum_{i\neq j}|u_{ij}|^{2}=1
. Fixing
p\in[1,\infty)
and picking an even
integer
l>p
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigg\|\sum_{i\neq j}u_{ij}\mathsf{x}_{ij}\Bigg\|_{p}^{l}\leq\mathbb{E}\phi\Bigg(\Bigg[\Bigg(\sum_{i\neq j}u_{ij}\mathsf{x}_{ij}\Bigg)\Bigg(\sum_{i\neq j}\bar{u}_{ij}\mathsf{x}_{ij}^{*}\Bigg)\Bigg]^{l/2}\Bigg)=\sum_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}}u_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}}\mathbb{E}\phi(\mathsf{x}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}})

where

\displaystyle(\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j})=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{l},j_{1},\ldots,j_{l}),\quad\quad\sum_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}}=\sum_{i_{1}\neq j_{1}}\cdots\sum_{i_{l}\neq j_{l}}

\displaystyle u_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}}=u_{i_{1}j_{1}}\bar{u}_{i_{2}j_{2}}\cdots u_{i_{l-1}j_{l-1}}\bar{u}_{i_{l}j_{l}},\quad\quad\mathsf{x}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}}=\mathsf{x}_{i_{1}j_{1}}\mathsf{x}_{i_{2}j_{2}}^{*}\cdots\mathsf{x}_{i_{l-1}j_{l-1}}\mathsf{x}_{i_{l}j_{l}}^{*}=\prod_{a=1}^{l}\widetilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i_{a}j_{a}}.

Define
T\equiv T(\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j})
as the indices that appear exactly once in
the combined index list
(\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j})=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{l},j_{1},\ldots,j_{l})
. Then,
expanding

\mathsf{x}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}}=\sum_{S_{1},\ldots,S_{l}\subseteq T}\mathsf{x}(S_{1},\ldots,S_{l})=\sum_{S_{1},\ldots,S_{l}\subseteq T}\prod_{a=1}^{l}\mathbb{E}_{T\setminus S_{a}}\mathcal{Q}_{S_{a}}[\widetilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i_{a},j_{a}}],

the same arguments as above using the conditions
\mathbb{E}_{i_{a}}[\widetilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i_{a},j_{a}}]=\mathbb{E}_{j_{a}}[\widetilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i_{a},j_{a}}]=0
and (
41
) show

•

|\mathbb{E}\phi(\mathsf{x}(S_{1},\ldots,S_{l}))|\leq n^{-\alpha l-\sum_{a=1}^{l}|S_{a}\setminus\{i_{a},j_{a}\}|/2+\epsilon}
.

•

If
i_{a}\in T
(or
j_{a}\in T
), then
\mathsf{x}(S_{1},\ldots,S_{l})=0
unless

i_{a}\in S_{a}
(resp.
j_{a}\in S_{a}
).

•

If
i_{a}\in T
(or
j_{a}\in T
), then
\mathbb{E}\phi(\mathsf{x}(S_{1},\ldots,S_{l}))=0

unless furthermore
i_{a}\in S_{b}
(resp.
j_{a}\in S_{b}
) for some
b\neq a
.

Thus
\sum_{a=1}^{l}|S_{a}\setminus\{i_{a},j_{a}\}|\geq|T|
, so we obtain
similarly as part (b)

\mathbb{E}\left\|\sum_{i\neq j}u_{ij}\mathsf{x}_{ij}\right\|_{p}^{l}\leq C_{l}n^{-\alpha l+\epsilon}\sum_{\pi}n^{-|T(\pi)|/2}\sum_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}:\pi(\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j})=\pi}|u_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}}|

where
\pi
is the partition of
\{1,\ldots,2l\}
induced by coincident indices
of the combined list
(\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j})
, and
|T(\pi)|
is the number of singleton
blocks of
\pi
. Lemma
C.1
applied with

B_{ij}^{a}=\begin{cases}|u_{ij}|&\text{ if }i\neq j\\
0&\text{ if }i=j\end{cases}\qquad\text{ for all }a=1,\ldots,l

shows
\sum_{\pi:|T(\pi)|=t}\sum_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}:\pi(\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j})=\pi}|u_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}}|\leq C_{l}n^{t/2}
. Then the proof of part (c) is concluded by the same Markov
inequality argument as in part (a-b).
∎

Appendix D
von Neumann algebras and non-commutative
L^{p}
spaces

We collect here several pieces of background on von Neumann algebras and
non-commutative
L^{p}
spaces that are needed in our main arguments. We refer to

[
50
]
,
[
14
, Section 1]
,
and
[
58
, Chapter 14]
for additional discussion.
Throughout,
\mathcal{X}
is a (finite) von Neumann algebra with faithful, normal,
tracial state
\phi
as in Section
3
.

Lemma D.1

(Conditional expectation.

[
10
]
, Lemma 1.5.11)
.

Let
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{X}
be a von Neumann subalgebra.
Then there exists a unique linear map
\phi^{\mathcal{B}}:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{B}

(the
\phi
-invariant conditional expectation) that satisfies the following:

•

\phi^{\mathcal{B}}
is normal, contractive in the operator norm,
and completely positive.

•

For any
\mathsf{y}_{1},\mathsf{y}_{2}\in\mathcal{B}
and
\mathsf{x}\in\mathcal{X}
, we have

\phi^{\mathcal{B}}[\mathsf{y}_{1}\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}_{2}]=\mathsf{y}_{1}\phi^{\mathcal{B}}[\mathsf{x}]\mathsf{y}_{2}
.

•

For any
\mathsf{y}\in\mathcal{B}
,
\phi(\mathsf{y})=\phi(\phi^{\mathcal{B}}[\mathsf{y}])
.

Defining
\|\mathsf{x}\|_{p}=\phi(|x|^{p})^{1/p}
, the space
L^{p}(\mathcal{X})
is the
Banach space completion of
\mathcal{X}
under
\|\cdot\|_{p}
. We set

\|\mathsf{x}\|_{\infty}\equiv\|\mathsf{x}\|_{\mathrm{op}}
and
L^{\infty}(\mathcal{X})\equiv\mathcal{X}
. These spaces

L^{p}(\mathcal{X})
may be continuously embedded into a common space of (unbounded)
densely-defined operators affiliated to
\mathcal{X}
— we refer to

[
50
]
or
[
14
, Section 1]
for this
construction.

Lemma D.2

(Non-commutative
L^{p}
-spaces.

[
58
]
Theorem 14.1,

[
14
]
Proposition 1.1)
.

For each
p\in[1,\infty)
,
\|\mathsf{x}\|_{p}=\phi(|x|^{p})^{1/p}
defines a complete
norm on
L^{p}(\mathcal{X})
, satisfying

\|\mathsf{x}\|_{p}\leq\|\mathsf{y}\|_{p}\text{ for all }\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y}\in L^{p}(\mathcal{X})\text{ with }0\leq\mathsf{x}\leq\mathsf{y},\qquad|\phi(x)|\leq\|\mathsf{x}\|_{1}\text{ for all }\mathsf{x}\in L^{1}(\mathcal{X}).

(a)

(Hölder’s inequality)
For any
1\leq p,q,r\leq\infty
with
1/p+1/q=1/r
, these norms satisfy

\|\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}\|_{r}\leq\|\mathsf{x}\|_{p}\|\mathsf{y}\|_{q}\text{ for all }\mathsf{x}\in L^{p}(\mathcal{X}),\;\mathsf{y}\in L^{q}(\mathcal{X}).

In particular,
\|\mathsf{x}\|_{p}\leq\|\mathsf{x}\|_{q}
for any
1\leq p\leq q\leq\infty

so
L^{q}(\mathcal{X})\subseteq L^{p}(\mathcal{X})
, and
\|\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}\|_{p}\leq\|\mathsf{x}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|\mathsf{y}\|_{p}
.

(b)

(Duality) For each
p\in[1,\infty)
, let
q
be such that

\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1
. Then
the map
\mathsf{y}\in L^{q}\mapsto\ell_{\mathsf{y}}\in(L^{p})^{*}
given by

\ell_{\mathsf{y}}(\mathsf{x})=\phi(\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y})
is a Banach space isomorphism between

L^{q}
and the dual
(L^{p})^{*}
of
L^{p}
.

Lemma D.3

(
L^{p}
-contractivity of conditional expectation)
.

Let
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{X}
be a von Neumann subalgebra, and let
\phi^{\mathcal{B}}:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{B}
be the unique
\phi
-invariant conditional expectation. Then for any
p\in[1,\infty)
and
\mathsf{x}\in\mathcal{X}
,

\|\phi^{\mathcal{B}}(\mathsf{x})\|_{p}\leq\|\mathsf{x}\|_{p}.

Proof.

For
p=1
, let
\mathsf{y}\in\mathcal{B}
be the projection
operator for which
\mathsf{y}\phi^{\mathcal{B}}(\mathsf{x})=|\phi^{\mathcal{B}}(\mathsf{x})|
. Then

\|\phi^{\mathcal{B}}(\mathsf{x})\|_{1}=\phi(\mathsf{y}\phi^{\mathcal{B}}(\mathsf{x}))=\phi(\mathsf{y}\mathsf{x})\leq\|\mathsf{y}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|\mathsf{x}\|_{1}=\|\mathsf{x}\|_{1}.

Similarly for
p\in(1,\infty)
, by the density of
\mathcal{X}
in
L^{q}(\mathcal{X})
and
the above
L^{p}
-
L^{q}
duality on
\mathcal{X}
as well as
on its subalgebra
\mathcal{B}
,

\|\phi^{\mathcal{B}}(\mathsf{x})\|_{p}=\sup_{\mathsf{y}\in\mathcal{B}:\|\mathsf{y}\|_{q}=1}\phi(\mathsf{y}\phi^{\mathcal{B}}(\mathsf{x}))=\sup_{\mathsf{y}\in\mathcal{B}:\|\mathsf{y}\|_{q}=1}\phi(\mathsf{y}\mathsf{x})\leq\sup_{\mathsf{y}\in\mathcal{X}:\|\mathsf{y}\|_{q}=1}\phi(\mathsf{y}\mathsf{x})=\|\mathsf{x}\|_{p}.

∎

Lemma D.4

(Riesz-Thorin interpolation.
[
14
]
,
Proposition 1.6)
.

Suppose, for some
p_{0},q_{0},p_{1},q_{1}\in[1,\infty]
, that

T:\mathcal{X}\to L^{q_{0}}(\mathcal{X},\phi)\cap L^{q_{1}}(\mathcal{X},\phi)
is a linear map
satisfying

\|T\mathsf{x}\|_{q_{0}}\leq M_{0}\|\mathsf{x}\|_{p_{0}},\qquad\|T\mathsf{x}\|_{q_{1}}\leq M_{1}\|\mathsf{x}\|_{p_{1}}

for all
\mathsf{x}\in\mathcal{X}
and some
M_{0},M_{1}>0
. If

\frac{1}{p_{\theta}}=\frac{1-\theta}{p_{1}}+\frac{\theta}{p_{2}}

and
\frac{1}{q_{\theta}}=\frac{1-\theta}{q_{1}}+\frac{\theta}{q_{2}}
,
then for all
\mathsf{x}\in\mathcal{X}
,

\|T\mathsf{x}\|_{q_{\theta}}\leq M_{0}^{1-\theta}M_{1}^{\theta}\|\mathsf{x}\|_{p_{\theta}}.
